# SAIRN Open Work Index

**One place to see everything that is open across the whole platform.**
Last rebuilt: **2026-08-23**. Last updated: **2026-09-04** (SAIRNscape's `scpLd()` fixed, 7 silent loaders left; StoneDesk's public surface has no tables; SAIRNroofing's published-PIN row closed and re-verified live).

Before this file existed, open work lived in `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`, in
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md`, in ~40 per-app handoffs, and in skill files — so real
items got missed and sessions sat idle while work existed. This is the index.
It does not replace those files; each row points back at the source of truth.

**Note, 2026-08-24:** `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md` was split per session to stop the
recurring merge conflicts. Its historical entries are still there (unchanged),
but new active-work entries go in `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-hank.md`,
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-cc.md`, `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-cody.md`, and
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-fourth.md` (one per clone). **The next rebuild of this index
must read all four, not just the shared file.**

---

## How to use this in 30 seconds

1. Filter by **Owner** — `unassigned` rows are the ones a free session can take.
2. Skip anything whose **Blocked by** is not empty unless you can clear the block.
3. Do what **Next action** says. If it says *verify*, the row's status is a claim
   that has not been re-checked; verify before building on it.

**Every row is a claim, not a fact.** Same standard as a handoff: re-verify
before you act on one. Rows marked ⚠️ are ones this rebuild could not confirm.

Nobody is losing a working feature; there was never a working feature. **Next action: run `sql/stonedesk_public_surface_schema.sql`.** It is additive and idempotent, creates all five tables (`sd_public_shop`, `sd_quote_requests`, `sd_customers`, `sd_order_links`, `sd_public_rate_limits`), enables RLS with service-role-only policies, and its grants already include the `update` that the counter's `merge-duplicates` upsert needs at plan time — the exact verb whose absence took `sairncash_waitlist` down for hours on 2026-08-26. `tools/sairn_sql_preflight.py` reports it clean, **which is not a pass** — that tool compares against the repo's own CREATE TABLE statements, so a file written and never run looks PRESENT to it, which is precisely this case. **Confirm by re-probing the two endpoints after the run, not by the editor reporting success** | S |

(3) **Named carve-out and the sweep's sharpest risk:** `intake_submissions` must keep `anon`'s SELECT/UPDATE/DELETE or StoneDesk's intake panel breaks silently — assert it before and after, not just after. Its blast radius is **not knowable from this repo**, because Supabase internals (Studio, Realtime, PostgREST schema-cache reload) may touch `public` as those roles in ways no file here describes. Everything else is verifiable from the catalog; that one is not. Reversal is a single `GRANT`, so the cost is low, but the failure mode could be non-obvious and land somewhere nobody is watching. **RECOMMENDED: SPLIT IT.** Stage A — tables, sequences, routines, and the widened default privileges: no operational unknowns, fully verifiable, fold into the existing file. Stage B — the schema `usage` revoke alone, its own run, its own window, its own verification, so that if something does break the cause is unambiguous. **Do not run them together**; a combined failure would leave nobody able to say which half caused it. One further cost worth naming: if a public intake form is ever built the right way, `anon` needs `INSERT` on one table, and Stage B makes restoring that two steps rather than one. Small, acceptable, but it should be a known cost rather than a surprise. **STAGE A RAN AND VERIFIED 2026-08-26** — see the sweep row above. **STAGE B RUN PLAN, one statement, its own window:** `revoke usage on schema public from anon, authenticated;`. Before-state is already recorded by 3f (both roles hold `USAGE`). **Rollback is one line and should be in the clipboard before the revoke is run, not looked up afterwards:** `grant usage on schema public to anon, authenticated;`. **Verify immediately after, in this order:** (1) re-run 3f, expect zero rows; (2) a real `api/sd-data.js` read through the proxy — that path runs as `service_role` and must be **unaffected**, so a failure there is the signal to roll back at once; (3) load the Supabase dashboard's table view, which is the part no query can predict and the reason this statement is not sharing a run with anything else. **SEQUENCING RECOMMENDATION — run the `supabase_admin` check FIRST** (the row above, read-only, minutes). It is independent of Stage B, but if it comes back live then a second granting role is handing `anon` full CRUD on new tables, and closing schema `USAGE` while that door is open is the smaller of the two jobs. Rollback is in the header to be opened *before* Section 2. Section 4 carries Stage A's held cleanup **and settles the count-drift prediction**: take the table count, drop both baselines, take it again — it must fall by **exactly 2**. Same shape as the `dnt_providers` `linked_employee_id` refusal in the same handler. **THE COMPARISON MIRRORS `lookupCoverage()` EXACTLY, and that is the point rather than a detail:** payer trimmed and lower-cased, `procedure_type_id` compared STRICTLY with no coercion. Looser than the reader and it refuses rules the app treats as distinct; tighter and it certifies "no conflict" while the reader still collides. A numeric `procedure_type_id` deliberately does not clash with the string form, because `lookupCoverage()` would not match them either. **FAIL-CLOSED, a deliberate divergence from the `dnt_providers` precedent it otherwise copies.** That one wraps its read in `if (dupR.ok)` and lets the write through when the check cannot run; this returns **503 COVERAGE_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE**, following the newer GFE precedent in the same file, because a uniqueness check that silently did not run is indistinguishable from one that passed. A 404/400 is NOT treated as a failure — the table simply does not exist, so there is nothing to duplicate and the write answers the standard `NOT_PROVISIONED`. **THE CLIENT HAD TO MOVE WITH IT, and that is the half that would have been missed:** `addCoverageRule()` wrote to localStorage BEFORE calling the server and toasted *"Saved on this device only — server sync not yet enabled for this app"* on any failure. Reordered server-first, same as `addProvider()`, and it now shows the server's own words via `dntLastErrText()`. **Cost, stated:** adding a coverage rule now requires the server; a network failure means the rule is not added rather than added locally. Both suites strip whitespace before matching now, with one negative control per spelling. If a remaining app is spelled differently again, add a NORMALISED pattern, not a third literal. **AND ADDING AN APP TO THE SUITE IS NOT THE SAME AS COVERING IT** — on 2026-09-04 a scripted edit inserted two apps after an anchor another session had just changed, `String.replace` returned the string unchanged, and the suite went green at a HIGHER assertion count because a third app had arrived in the same window. A rising count read as coverage. Assert the anchor matched, count the printed section headings, and trust the negative control over the total. **SAIRNscape then found a THIRD vacuity of the same family, this time in the FUNCTION NAME rather than the body:** its loader is `scpLd()`, and the suite hardcoded `function ld(k,d){` in both the driver and the source assertion. Adding the app unchanged would have produced a green section that drove nothing and a source check true of a file it had never looked at — the sixth app reading as progress. The name is derived from the app row now, and each source pattern carries a negative control asserting it still matches the defect it names. Its body was also the **truthiness** spelling `r ? JSON.parse(r) : d`, SAIRNbiz's, so an empty string took the absent branch silently; `scpSv()` JSON.stringify()s and can never write `''`, so that value did not come from the app and now reads as unreadable. Return unchanged at all 44 call sites. **Do not do the remaining five in one pass** — that is the same mistake the fifteen-resource validation row warns about, and the tool reports them so the list can be burned down rather than swept | M |

| **Tooling** | ~~`git_push_master_guard` denies on the SUBSTRING `master`, including inside a filename~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `2c1ed4c0` — anchored on the destination refspec, live-verified in BOTH directions through the real hook | Cody | — | **CLOSED, and the fix was not where the row said it was.** The guard now splits the command on shell separators, finds the `git [global-opts] push` invocation in each piece, and compares only the DESTINATION half of its refspecs. Measured against the 27-case probe: the old rule wrongly DENIED 6 of 14 legitimate commands and wrongly ALLOWED 1 of 13 hazards — `git -C <path> push origin master` slipped the old regex entirely, so this is a net tightening rather than only a loosening. Three shapes are in the deny list specifically because a plausible version of this change would have let them through: separators are split on the raw TEXT rather than shlex's tokens (shlex splits on whitespace only, so `...origin master;echo hi` would arrive as one token comparing unequal); `--force-with-lease` is deliberately NOT in the consumes-a-value list, because listing it would make the parser eat the remote and never read the ref; and an unparseable piece falls back to the OLD blunt substring test rather than to allow, so a parse failure cannot become a hole. Three boundaries are stated in the module header rather than left to look like misses: a bare `git push` with no refspec is not inspected, the SOURCE half is not checked, and `archive/master` stays allowed. **THE HALF THAT MATTERED MORE, found only by trying to commit the fix: editing the repo copy did not change what runs.** The commit was denied by the OLD rule with the new code already on disk. `C:/Users/marsh/.claude/settings.json` — the GLOBAL file, not any clone's — still points four hooks at `C:/SAIRN/tools/`, and its copy of this guard was byte-identical to the pre-fix version. Both hooks run and a deny from either blocks. The external copy has been synced so live behaviour matches the reviewed code; **the duplicate global entries are left for Michael** (see the row below on the same directory) because that file governs every project on this machine. **Live-verified both ways after the push:** a command line containing `git push` followed by the word was ALLOWED and the push completed, and `git push --dry-run origin master` was DENIED. Held by `tests/run_push_master_guard_probe.py`. Original finding below. `tools/git_push_master_guard.py:28` was `re.search(r'git push.*master', cmd)`. **The `.*` spans the whole command string**, so any occurrence of the word anywhere after a push invocation trips it — including in an unrelated argument, a commit message, or a path. **Reproduced THREE times by accident, not theorised:** a diagnostic line that pushed to `HEAD:main` and separately named the file `git_push_master_guard.py` was DENIED with *"this command pushes to the stale 'master' branch"*, the only occurrence of the word being the guard's own filename; the command that first tried to ADD THIS ROW was denied for the same reason, because the row text quotes both halves of the pattern; and then the COMMIT of that row was denied too, because the commit message did. The row listed a commit message as a hypothetical before the third one made it an observation. **This is a false positive, not a hole, and the direction matters** — the guard cannot be talked into ALLOWING a push to the stale branch, only into refusing something harmless. That is why it was left alone rather than fixed inside an unrelated change. **Fix shape, not chosen:** anchor on the refspec rather than the line — match the word only where a ref can appear (`<remote> [+]<src>:<dst>`, or a bare ref), which is the same parsing `sairn_push_gate_hook.pushed_tip()` already does and could be reused. **Do NOT loosen it to a bare word match** — a commit message or a path would then decide, which is the same class of defect as the push gate's override matcher firing on a quoted mention (found and fixed 2026-09-03). Cost so far: three denied commands, each retried by routing the text through a file so no command line carries the pattern. Cost if ignored: a session hits it on a real push and reaches for an override it does not need, which is how a gate gets hollowed out | S |

| **SAIRNlaw** | **"Which holidays actually count" — ONE bundled lawyer's question across four jurisdictions, not four separate ones** | Open — legal judgment | **Michael** | Needs a lawyer's read | **Bundled on Michael's direction 2026-08-25: do this as one research/decision pass, not one state at a time as each is seeded.** Every jurisdiction hits some version of it and the answers are likely to share reasoning. **(1) ILLINOIS** — statute (205 ILCS 630/17) vs court-observed (M.R. 5272) disagree **both ways**; M.R. 5272 omits Pulaski Day and Good Friday and adds the day after Thanksgiving. Confirm the statutory list is the right basis for 5 ILCS 70/1.11. Encoded that way; changes real dates. **(2) KENTUCKY — the blocker that stops the state being seeded at all.** RAP 6(A) and KRS 446.030 both say "legal holiday" and neither defines it; KRS 2.110 is titled "Public holidays", never uses the phrase, lists four days KY courts do not close for (Robert E. Lee Day, F.D. Roosevelt Day, Lincoln's Birthday, Confederate Memorial Day) and omits Thanksgiving. Encoding it fails **LATE**. **(3) TEXAS** — Tex. R. Civ. P. 4 and Tex. R. App. P. 4.1 both say "legal holiday" without defining it or citing Tex. Gov't Code 662.021, which is what the `tx` calendars encode; the link is the settled reading, not an express cross-reference. **(4) WEST VIRGINIA** — R. Civ. P. 6(a)(6) and R. App. P. 39(a) define it **differently within the same state** (Juneteenth), and one calendar per jurisdiction cannot serve both. **(5) ARIZONA** (added 2026-08-25) — Ariz. R. Civ. P. 6(a) says "legal holiday" and, on the text read, names no statute; the candidate A.R.S. 1-301 was not read. Same shape as Texas. **TWO JURISDICTIONS HAVE NOW ANSWERED IT THEMSELVES AND THEY ANSWER IT DIFFERENTLY, which is the strongest argument for asking this once rather than per state: WASHINGTON's CR 6(a) says outright "Legal holidays are prescribed in RCW 1.16.050" — an express cross-reference — and that statute then excludes its own decoys in subsection (7) ("may not be considered legal holidays for any purpose"); NORTH CAROLINA's Rule 6(a) instead asks whether "the courthouse is closed for transactions", so its statute is the wrong source entirely.** So the answer is not uniform and the question is really: which source does THIS rule's own words point at, and what do you do when they point at nothing? The common question: **when a court rule says "legal holiday" and points at nothing, does the general holiday statute supply the list, even where that statute is broader or narrower than what the courts actually observe?** Add the two Texas secondary-source rows to the same set while a lawyer is engaged **CATEGORY ADDED 2026-08-26, on Michael’s direction, covering MARYLAND and WISCONSIN together rather than as two new categories — they are the SAME SHAPE: a plausible-looking source that fails in the DANGEROUS direction.** Every jurisdiction above asks *which* list supplies “legal holiday”. These two ask something sharper: **the obvious source is not merely uncertain, it is affirmatively WRONG, and using it rolls deadlines LATE.** **(6) MARYLAND** — the intuitive path (Gen. Provisions § 1-302 + § 1-111) is wrong in BOTH directions at once: § 1-302 rolls only off Sunday, with no Saturday roll (→ EARLY), while § 1-111 adds Good Friday, Lincoln’s Birthday, Maryland Day and Defenders’ Day, four days that are NOT court holidays (→ LATE). Rule 1-203’s own committee note settles it — it **supersedes § 1-302**. Correct chain: Rule 1-203 → Rule 1-202(l) → **State Personnel & Pensions § 9-201**. **(7) WISCONSIN** — the wrongness is not which statute but **which TEST**. § 801.15(1)(b) rolls the last day unless it is “a day the clerk of courts office is closed” (a CLOSURE test), while the composed holiday definition in (1)(a) governs ONLY the sub-11-day intermediate-day exclusion. So the obvious move — drive the rollover off the holiday list — is the wrong basis, and because Wisconsin’s clerks of circuit court are COUNTY officers, any clerk open on a listed day means the rollover fires when the statute would not: **LATE**. The 1985 Law Revision Committee Note makes it worse rather than better, saying the amendment “will **permit** clerks to close their offices at these times” — permit, not require. **WHY THESE TWO BELONG WITH THE OTHERS AND NOT IN A ROW OF THEIR OWN:** the answer a lawyer gives to “when a rule says legal holiday and points at nothing, does the general statute supply the list?” is the same reasoning that answers “and what if the general statute is affirmatively the wrong one?” Splitting them would invite two inconsistent answers. **Both states are GATED-PASSED but deliberately UNSEEDED pending this** — see their own rows. Contrast the two that resolved safely: **MISSOURI**, where RSMo 9.010 is titled “Public holidays” (the identical lexical gap that helped kill Kentucky) but converges day-for-day with the state’s own schedule, so both readings agree; and **MASSACHUSETTS**, whose Rule 6(a) names its statute expressly. The distinction the lawyer needs to draw is CONVERGES vs DIVERGES, not NAMED vs UNNAMED. | L |

Missouri (~6.2M), largest unseeded after Tennessee failed. **Rules** free and official at `courts.mo.gov` with real per-rule currency (44.01 revised eff. **2025-01-01**, 43.01 eff. 2018-07-01, 103.08 eff. 2020-07-01). **Statutes are the strongest of any state gated:** `revisor.mo.gov` is the official Revisor, free, **HTTP 200 to plain curl**, no challenge, no terms gate, no publisher redirect, and it prints **per-section version history with effective dates** — the exact opposite of Tennessee, which failed on a publisher terms gate. **ACCESS QUIRK TO RECORD:** `courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=…` sits behind a **Cloudflare bot challenge** that neither curl nor headless Playwright clears, but the underlying **`ClerkHandbooksP2RulesOnly.nsf/…` document URLs are NOT challenged** and return full text — a challenge on `page.jsp` does NOT mean the rule is unavailable. Third distinct access mechanism after mass.gov and tncourts.gov. **THE HOLIDAY GAP IS KENTUCKY-SHAPED IN WORDING BUT NOT IN RISK:** Rule 44.01(a) says "legal holiday" and names no statute, and RSMo 9.010 is titled "**Public** holidays" — the identical lexical gap that helped kill Kentucky. It comes out the opposite way: Kentucky's KRS 2.110 lists four days its courts do NOT close for and omits Thanksgiving (fails **LATE**), whereas **RSMo 9.010's thirteen days match the State of Missouri's own 2026 schedule day for day** (verified against `oa.mo.gov`), including Lincoln's Birthday 12 Feb and Truman Day 8 May, and Thanksgiving is present. Both candidate readings converge, so encoding 9.010 cannot roll LATE on any listed day. **Add to the bundled lawyer's question as a NEW category** — rule says "legal holiday", points at nothing, obvious statute is titled something else but converges in practice — distinct from TX/AZ (convergence unknown) and KY (diverges dangerously). **ONE REAL DIVERGENCE, FAILS EARLY:** 9.010's shift is **SUNDAY-ONLY** (silent on Saturday, like Massachusetts, unlike VA/WV) yet the administration observes **Fri 3 July 2026** for a Saturday 4 July. Encode the statute and DISCLOSE the observed substitutes — encoding them instead would roll LATE. **DO NOT add a Saturday shift by analogy to VA/WV; that is the second state running where it would be wrong.** | L |

| **SAIRNlaw** | **STANDING GATE CHECK: the weekend-coverage question — run it on every future jurisdiction** | Open (reference, run every gate) | unassigned | — | **Promoted to a standing check on Michael’s direction 2026-08-26**, after four consecutive states hit it and gave four different answers. **THE OLD QUESTION WAS “does the computation rule name Saturday and Sunday?” THAT IS THE WRONG QUESTION.** Several jurisdictions put the weekend INSIDE the holiday list rather than beside it, so a rule that names only “legal holiday” may still roll weekends — or may not. **THE CHECK, IN ORDER:** (1) does the rollover clause name Saturday and Sunday expressly? If yes, done — `isWeekend()` is correct. (2) If not, what does it key on — a holiday STATUTE, or COURTHOUSE CLOSURE? (3) If a statute: **does that statute make BOTH weekend days holidays, EVERYWHERE in the state?** Statewide matters as much as both-days. (4) If closure: the holiday list is the WRONG basis for the rollover and encoding it risks rolling **LATE** — get the judiciary’s closure schedule instead. **THE FOUR ANSWERS SO FAR:** **LOUISIANA** — rule says “legal holiday” only; R.S. 1:55 makes **Sundays** statewide but **Saturday a holiday in enumerated parishes only** → `isWeekend()` rolls Saturdays where the law does not → **LATE → BLOCKED**, and it is why the weekend-flag engine row exists. **OKLAHOMA** — same rule shape, but 25 O.S. § 82.1(A) opens “**Each Saturday, Sunday**”, statewide, no county qualification → **safe**. **OREGON** — ORS 187.010(1)(a) makes “Each Sunday” a holiday (the Louisiana quirk) but ORCP 10 A names **“a Saturday or a legal holiday, including Sunday”**, so the RULE saves it → **safe**. **CONNECTICUT** — no holiday list exists in the Practice Book at all (five “holiday” mentions in 699 pages, none definitional); the rollover keys purely on **clerk’s-office closure** → weekends are right by accident of fact, but the statutory list is the wrong basis and would risk LATE → **the Wisconsin hazard**. **WHY IT IS WORTH ITS OWN ROW:** the failure is silent. Every one of these produces plausible dates; only Louisiana’s is wrong, and only because a proviso buried mid-sentence scopes Saturday to some parishes. Nothing in a green test run would surface it. **FIFTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — UTAH, and it is the first to stop at STEP 1.** URCP 6(a)(1)(C) names both days inside the rollover clause itself: “if the last day is a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday, the period continues to run until the end of the next day that is not a **Saturday, Sunday** or legal holiday.” No holiday statute has to be consulted for the weekend question at all and there is no statewide-coverage sub-question — `isWeekend()` is correct on the rule’s own words. **SIXTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — IOWA, AND IT BREAKS THE CHECK ITSELF.** Every previous state had ONE answer. Iowa has TWO, and which applies depends on WHAT KIND OF DEADLINE IT IS. The rules contain no counting mechanics at all — R. Civ. P. 1.1801 is one sentence pointing at **Iowa Code § 4.1(34)** (`first day` 0 hits, `legal holiday` 0 hits, `Sunday` 0 hits across 130 pages). § 4.1(34) sentence one rolls **ONLY SUNDAY**; sentence two rolls **Saturday + Sunday + clerk-closure + 11 named holidays** but is scoped to “**the commencement of an action … the FILING of a pleading or motion … or the perfecting or filing of an appeal**”. **Iowa’s answer and discovery deadlines are SERVICE deadlines** — R. 1.303(1) “shall **serve** … within 20 days”, and R. 1.509 says outright “**Answers shall not be filed**; however, they shall be **served**”. On the words, sentence two may not reach them → rolling a Saturday would be **LATE by two days**. **CONSEQUENCE FOR THE ENGINE CHANGE BELOW: a per-JURISDICTION weekend flag is NOT enough for Iowa — it needs PER-RULE.** Read the Iowa gate before building that flag. **SEVENTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — NEVADA, safe at step 1 like Utah.** NRCP 6(a)(1)(C): “if the last day is a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday, the period continues to run until the end of the next day that is not a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday.” **EIGHTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — ARKANSAS, safe at step 1.** ARCP 6(a) names both days: “unless it is a **Saturday, Sunday**, legal holiday, or other day when the clerk’s office is closed”. Its clerk’s-office limb appears TWICE — as a rollover TRIGGER and as the rollover TARGET (“the next day that the clerk’s office is open”) — but both are ADDITIONAL to the named days, so both omissions run EARLY. Minnesota’s shape, not Wisconsin’s or Connecticut’s. **Running tally: LA blocked, OK safe (statute), OR safe (rule), CT premise-fails, UT safe at step 1, IA SPLIT WITHIN THE STATE → BLOCKED, NV safe at step 1, AR safe at step 1** | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Arkansas GATED — rules PASS and current to 4 June 2026, but the HOLIDAY STATUTE has no reachable primary source; one statute is the only blocker** | **Blocked 2026-08-27** ⚠ | unassigned | Ark. Code Ann. § 1-5-101 on an official source | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-arkansas-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Arkansas (~3.1M). **RULES PASS AND STATE THEIR OWN CURRENCY** — the ARCP PDF opens “Rules of Civil Procedure (**current through June 4, 2026**)” and the collection index prints the same date per rule set, which only Nevada has matched. **THE PATH IS NOT OBVIOUS, RECORDED SO IT IS NOT REDISCOVERED:** Arkansas serves rules from a **Lexum/Decisia portal**; `arcourts.gov/rules`, `/court-rules` and `/rules-and-administrative-orders` all **404**, `arkansasjudiciary.gov` and `courts.arkansas.gov` **do not resolve at all**, and `opinions.arcourts.gov/ark/en/nav.do` returns 200 with the rule links **JS-loaded and absent from the HTML**. The working route is **`?iframe=true`**: `/ark/en/nav.do?iframe=true` → `/ark/cr/en/nav_date.do?iframe=true` → **`/ark/cr/en/16712/1/document.do`** (1.3 MB PDF). Browser UA required. **⚠ THE BLOCKER IS ONE STATUTE.** Rule 6(a) defines “legal holiday” by pointing outward, so no calendar without **Ark. Code Ann. § 1-5-101** — and **`arkleg.state.ar.us` answers the statute’s FTPDocument path with HTTP 200 and a 42-byte 1×1 GIF** (`content-type: image/gif`). **FOURTH DISTINCT “200 THAT IS NOT CONTENT” SHAPE IN THREE DAYS** — after Utah’s error-page body, Iowa’s inert dated URL and njcourts’ anti-bot interstitial — **and the most easily missed: a fetch checking only status and non-zero length passes it.** Justia has the section but is secondary and self-disclaims. **EVERYTHING BELOW IS FROM JUSTIA AND IS NOT PRIMARY-SOURCED.** **THE HOLIDAY BASIS IS A UNION OF TWO LISTS** — first jurisdiction needing both. 6(a): holidays designated **by the President or Congress OR by the laws of this State**, and the Reporter’s Notes say the recitation was deliberately omitted as redundant. **THREE TRAPS:** (1) **CHRISTMAS EVE, 24 DECEMBER, is a state holiday** — no seeded state has it, and it is ADJACENT to Christmas, so when the 25th is a Sunday you get the 23rd AND the 26th around a weekend; (2) **the state list has NO Juneteenth and NO Columbus Day but the UNION does** — a calendar from the state statute alone omits two days, one from the federal list alone omits Christmas Eve, **neither list is correct on its own**; (3) **“an employee’s birthday” is a listed holiday and is NOT A CALENDAR DATE AT ALL** — unmodellable, omitting it runs EARLY. Observation shifts agree between the two lists (Sat→preceding Fri, Sun→succeeding Mon), and Arkansas’s is GENERAL rather than enumerated, unlike NV’s and UT’s. **SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION IS FOURTEEN DAYS — A NEW NUMBER AND THE LONGEST IN THE PLATFORM** (six states use 7, three use 11, MN/UT/NV have none). Arkansas **did NOT follow the federal 2009 abolition** — it went 11 → 14 while FRCP went to zero, so do not reason from FRCP 6(a) despite the Reporter’s Notes calling Rule 6 “practically identical to FRCP 6”. **RULE 6(d) IS THREE *BUSINESS* DAYS** (engine already supports the unit — `ny_cplr_2103b` uses it) **AND E-SERVICE IS EXPRESSLY INCLUDED**, the opposite of NV/WV/NY/federal — a copied allowlist would UNDER-count. **✅ AND THE ANSWER CARVE-OUT IS EXPRESS, WHICH CONFIRMS THE FEDERAL FIX MADE THE SAME DAY:** 6(d) ends “**this subdivision shall not extend the time in which the defendant must file an answer or pre-answer motion when service of the summons and complaint is by mail or commercial delivery company in accordance with Rule 4**” — Arkansas says outright what FRCP 6(d) leaves to inference and what two federal rows had got wrong. Note it is NARROWER: it excludes the answer only for mail/commercial-delivery service. **SEEDABLE ONCE CLEAR:** 12(a)(1) has **four** periods — 30 days; 30 from first publication of a warning order; **60 days for a defendant INCARCERATED in this state** (a party-status period no seeded state has); 30 for cross/counterclaim — plus 6(c)’s 20-day **backward** motion row and 10/5-day response/reply rows, **both of which fall under the 14-day exclusion**. **ALL THREE DISCOVERY RULES CARRY THE SAME FLOOR WITH THE SAME NUMBERS** — later of 30 from the request and 45 from the summons — which is the Georgia `resolve_periods` shape, and Arkansas would be its **third real user**; unusual uniformity, where MO is 45/45/60, NC 45/45/60, MN 45/45/none, MA none/45/45. **UPDATE 2026-08-27 — TWO OFFICIAL SOURCES FOUND ON MICHAEL’S LEADS; THE BLOCK IS NARROWED, NOT CLEARED, AND ARKANSAS STAYS GATED.** **THE GIF IS A PATH PROBLEM, NOT A DOMAIN PROBLEM — CONFIRMED:** `/Home/FTPDocument` serves real PDFs for Acts from **2017R onward** (150–235 KB) and the **1×1 GIF for 2015R and earlier**, a clean cut-off at 2017; the **ACA is not served under ANY path shape tried**, `/Home/ArkansasCode`, `/ACA` and the code search all **HTTP 500**, and the site’s own Arkansas Law portal links to Acts, Bills and the admin-rules site but **NOT to the Code** — consistent with Arkansas licensing the ACA commercially. **The Arkansas Code does not appear to be published free by the State.** **✅ PRIMARY 1 — ACT 561 OF 2017** (arkleg, 191 KB PDF): § 4 amends § 1-5-101(a)(2) to strike Robert E. Lee’s Birthday, leaving “Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr.’s Birthday — the third Monday in January”; § 5 moves Robert E. Lee Day to § 1-5-106 gubernatorial-proclamation memorial days (second Saturday in October), which is **NOT a holiday and must not enter a calendar**. **✅ PRIMARY 2 — THE TREASURER OF STATE’S SHEET** (`media.ark.org/artreasury/holidays_2025.pdf`, on the State’s own CDN): “2025 State Holidays — Legal holidays by authority of **Act 304 of 2001 and Amended by Act 561 of 2017**”, enumerating ten dated days that match Justia exactly and **independently confirming CHRISTMAS EVE**. It also **omits “an employee’s birthday”**, supporting reading that limb as personal leave rather than a calendar holiday. **⚠ WHY IT STILL BLOCKS: § 1-5-101(b), THE OBSERVATION SHIFT, IS STILL UNSOURCED FROM PRIMARY** — only Justia has Sat→preceding-Fri / Sun→succeeding-Mon, and **the Treasury sheet CANNOT corroborate it because NO fixed-date Arkansas holiday fell on a weekend in 2025** (1 Jan Wed, 4 Jul Fri, 11 Nov Tue, 24/25 Dec Wed/Thu) — it never had to apply the shift. **That is the load-bearing piece:** the shift is what makes the Christmas Eve / Christmas pair behave, and they straddle a weekend in **2027** (24th Fri, 25th Sat) and **2028** (24th Sun, 25th Mon), inside any range that would be generated. Also open: **whether any act after 2017 amended the section** — every biennium from 2017R is fetchable but arkleg’s act search is JS-driven and returns nothing in static HTML, so the history could not be swept, and Justia (the source being checked) is the only thing saying it stops at 2017. **THE REMAINING TASK IS NO LONGER “FIND THE STATUTE” BUT “SOURCE § 1-5-101(b)’S SHIFT AND CONFIRM NO POST-2017 AMENDMENT”.** `codeofarrules.arkansas.gov` was also tried — its search is JS-driven and returns an identical shell for every query. **UPDATE 2026-08-28 — ONE OF THE TWO QUESTIONS IS CLOSED. ✅ NOTHING AMENDED § 1-5-101 AFTER 2017:** Justia’s 2024 code page carries the full amendment chain back to 1943 and ends at Acts 2017 No. 561 § 4 — the act already read from primary. **What is relied on there is the HISTORY LINE, not the substantive text**, and the distinction is deliberate: a publisher’s citation list is the part of a secondary source least likely to be wrong, while the text is the part that gets subtly mis-rendered. A primary confirmation was attempted and is **not cheaply available** — sweeping 2019R–2025R for `1-5-101` is ~1,000 PDFs per session, and the index that would collapse it is not published (**every `CodeSectionsAmended` path returns the same 42-byte 1×1 GIF**; the HTML page is JS-driven). **⚠ STILL OPEN: § 1-5-101(b), THE OBSERVATION SHIFT.** Michael searched and found **four independent secondaries — Justia across multiple years, FindLaw, USLegal, Encyclopedia of Arkansas — all quoting IDENTICAL language**, plus one genuine primary hit on `codeofarrules.arkansas.gov` that **cites § 1-5-101 without containing it** (same category already ruled out; a citation is not the text). **Four secondaries agreeing is not a primary source and the block stands** — but the residual risk has changed shape: the realistic failure is no longer “the language is wrong” (four publishers do not independently invent one sentence) but “the language is stale”, which the closed question above makes unlikely too. **High confidence, no primary source, and the gate does not seed on confidence** — the same standard that keeps Iowa and Connecticut blocked. **WHAT WOULD CLEAR IT, by likely cost:** (1) the print or LexisNexis ACA (Arkansas licenses its Code, so probably the only route to codified text, and not free); (2) **the act that enacted (b)** — Act 561 amended only (a)(2), so (b) predates 2017, and **arkleg serves nothing before 2017R**, putting Act 304 of 2001 out of reach on that host; a different official archive of pre-2017 session laws would settle it; (3) a court or agency document that **QUOTES (b)** rather than citing the section — the Treasurer’s sheet was the near miss, applying the rule without stating it in a year that never had to | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Connecticut: SOURCES PASS but NOT seedable in the shape this engine models — the pleading rule is a CHAIN and the rollover has no holiday basis** | **Blocked 2026-08-26** ⚠️ | unassigned | Two structural questions, not source ones | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-connecticut-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Connecticut (~3.6M); rules of court are the **Practice Book**, adopted by the judges of the Superior Court. **SOURCES ARE AMONG THE CLEANEST GATED:** the Judicial Branch publishes the **OFFICIAL 2026 Practice Book** as one free PDF on plain curl — 3.6 MB, 699 pages, a **real text PDF not a scan**, first page reading “OFFICIAL / 2026 / CONNECTICUT PRACTICE BOOK” — with per-section amendment histories giving real `effective_from`; `cga.ct.gov` serves the General Statutes free too. Nothing here is the blocker. **BLOCKER ONE: SEC. 10-8 IS A CHAIN, NOT A DEADLINE.** Pleadings “shall advance within thirty days from the **return day**, and any subsequent pleadings … shall advance at least one step within each successive period of thirty days **from the preceding pleading** or the filing of the decision of the judicial authority thereon”. So the *n*th deadline depends on the *(n−1)*th ACTUAL FILING DATE, indefinitely. **Every seeded jurisdiction computes ONE date from ONE caller-supplied trigger.** Maryland’s chained discovery floor was logged as the highest-stakes shape found in any gate because ONE LIMB depended on another rule’s output; **Connecticut’s entire pleading cadence is that shape as the PRIMARY mechanism**. Seedable only as “30 days from the preceding pleading”, with a trigger named so nobody mistakes it for “30 days from the return day”, and a readme saying plainly that **Connecticut has no single answer deadline to compute**. Two domain-scoped variants ride the same sentence: **summary process 3 days, mortgage foreclosure 15 days**. And the **RETURN DAY is a Connecticut-specific concept fixed by Gen. Stat. § 52-48 (NOT read)** — it is a date stated in the writ, **not derivable from a service date**, so it must be caller-supplied and a seed must not silently equate the two. **BLOCKER TWO: THE ROLLOVER HAS NO HOLIDAY BASIS AT ALL.** Running the new standing weekend-coverage check, Connecticut fails the question’s premise: **“holiday” appears FIVE times in 699 pages and none of the five is definitional** (two are professional-conduct commentary on gifts, one is juvenile detention). Sec. 63-2 and Sec. 7-17 both roll on **“a day when the office of the clerk … is closed”**. Weekends therefore come out right by accident of fact (offices are shut), but **encoding Conn. Gen. Stat. § 1-4’s list for the rollover would risk rolling LATE if a clerk’s office is in fact open on a listed day — the Wisconsin hazard**. Sec. 7-17 says offices close “during weeks which include a legal holiday” but **never says which days**. Correct basis is the **Judicial Branch’s published court-closure schedule**, making Connecticut ingest-not-derive alongside NC, NJ, MD and OK; plus a discretionary limb (closure authorized by the administrative judge “due to the existence of special circumstances”) that is unknowable → EARLY. **NO INTERMEDIATE-DAY EXCLUSION, STATED AFFIRMATIVELY:** Sec. 63-2 — “**Time shall be counted by calendar, not working, days.**” So the field is ABSENT, and unlike Minnesota (where it had to be inferred from a days-are-days rule) Connecticut says it outright. **NO SERVICE EXTENSION FOUND ANYWHERE IN THE PRACTICE BOOK** — zero hits for “days shall be added”, “shall be added to the prescribed” and “additional time after service”. All nineteen seeded jurisdictions have one. **Reported as an absence searched for, NOT as a conclusion**: the General Statutes were not searched, and that is the single most important remaining read, because assuming “no extension” wrongly computes EARLY on every mailed period. **AN E-FILING CUTOFF THAT MOVES THE FILING, NOT THE DEADLINE:** Sec. 7-17 deems a document e-filed **after 5 p.m.** (or at any time on a closed day) filed on the next business day — mechanically Missouri’s completion rule but applied to **filing** rather than service, so it does not change the DATE this engine reports, only that a filer has until 5 p.m. rather than midnight. Row note, not a mechanism. **ALSO NOT READ:** Gen. Stat. § 1-4; the discovery periods (Secs. 13-6, 13-9, 13-22/13-23, located but not read); and the Judicial Branch closure schedule itself. **UPDATE 2026-08-27 — PARTIALLY SEEDED, DELIBERATELY UNPROVISIONED (Hank).** The chain row is now live in `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_connecticut.json` — ONE row, `ct-pb-10-8-advance-from-preceding-pleading`, trigger `filing_of_preceding_pleading` (named at length so it cannot be read as `return_day`), 30 calendar days forward, new standard `ct_pb_63_2` (`impl: frcp_6a`, **no** `short_period_exclusion_days`), `effective_from` **2014-01-01** from Sec. 10-8’s own amendment line. **NO `ct` CALENDAR WAS CREATED AND THAT IS THE HOLD:** `rollOff()` consults the calendar BEFORE it looks at the weekday, so Connecticut refuses `NOT_PROVISIONED` on **every** date, not just weekends — the Wisconsin hazard cannot be walked into. `api/_lib/deadline-connecticut.test.js` (26 assertions) asserts that on a **Tuesday** landing as well as a weekend one and **FAILS IF ANYONE LOADS A `ct` CALENDAR**. No `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` entry — that text rides on SUCCESSFUL computations, so one would be dormant (Guardian 0d); it goes in with the calendar. **RE-VERIFYING THE GATE AGAINST THE PDF FOUND THREE THINGS IT MISSED, one of which cuts the other way:** **(1) §3a, the only LATE-direction exposure —** the counting rule Sec. 63-2 sits under **RULES OF APPELLATE PROCEDURE**, and on de-hyphenated text `first day shall` occurs **exactly once in 699 pages**, there; `computation of time`, `computing any period`, `period of time prescribed` and `intermediate Saturdays` are **0 hits each**. There is NO trial-court counting rule. 63-2 arguably still reaches Superior Court (it governs “any documents … under these rules” and its own rollover names “the clerk of the **trial court**”) but that is not conclusive — and if it does not, the first-day convention is ASSUMED not sourced and computes **LATE**. Tolerable only because nothing computes; it now blocks the calendar alongside the closure schedule. **(2) §5a —** the no-service-extension finding survived a de-hyphenated re-run (0 hits, 5 patterns) AND got stronger: **Sec. 10-13 says “Service by mail is complete upon mailing”** — affirmative, not an absence, and mechanically Missouri’s completion shape. Its e-delivery limb carries an unknowable negative condition → EARLY. **(3) §4a —** Sec. 7-17 has a SECOND early limb §4 missed: e-filing **system-outage** tolling (nonoperational 30 consecutive min 9am–3pm, or ANY time 3–5pm). **NOT SEEDED and why:** the return-day first step (needs Gen. Stat. § 52-48; the return day is NOT derivable from a service date); the summary-process **3-day** and foreclosure **15-day** variants (real and quoted — held on a DOMAIN decision, `DOMAIN_LABELS` knows only `civil-litigation` and `appellate`, not on any doubt about the text); discovery, appellate and backward rows. **WHICH ITEM BLOCKS WHAT:** §3a and the closure schedule block the CALENDAR; § 52-48 blocks only the return-day row; a General-Statutes service extension blocks **nothing seeded** (this row runs from a FILING). 479/479 deadline tests pass. **⛔ HARD BLOCK, RESTATED EXPLICITLY ON MICHAEL’S DIRECTION 2026-08-27: DO NOT ADD A CONNECTICUT CALENDAR UNTIL THE FIRST-DAY-CONVENTION SOURCING QUESTION IS ANSWERED — DOING SO WOULD SILENTLY COMPUTE LATE.** The question in one sentence: **does Practice Book Sec. 63-2 reach Superior Court pleadings, or only appeals?** It is the sole source for “the last day shall, and the first day shall not, be counted” and it sits under RULES OF APPELLATE PROCEDURE. **This is a SECOND, INDEPENDENT blocker from the Wisconsin/closure-schedule hazard, and the more dangerous of the two** — every other disclosed Connecticut gap fails EARLY; this one fails LATE. **Answering the closure-schedule question alone does NOT unblock the calendar**, and shipping one on that basis is the specific mistake being guarded against: it would look like the hazard had been handled while the first-day convention was still assumed. Written into three places so it cannot be missed — the seed `_readme` opens with it, `ct_pb_63_2`’s comment block in `deadline-engine.js` carries it, and `deadline-connecticut.test.js` FAILS if a `ct` calendar appears at all | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Oklahoma gate PASSED — has Louisiana’s rollover SHAPE but not its problem, and the calendar is NOT fully derivable** | **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28** | unassigned | — | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-oklahoma-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Oklahoma (~4.1M), statutory civil procedure (Oklahoma Pleading Code, Title 12). **OSCN — the Oklahoma State Courts Network — publishes Titles 12 and 25 free on plain curl** with per-section Historical Data, and its title indexes list every section with its CiteID, so navigation is mechanical rather than guesswork. **IT HAS LOUISIANA’S SHAPE:** 12 O.S. § 2006(A)(1) rolls the last day only if it is “a legal holiday as defined by Section 82.1 of Title 25 … or any other day when the office of the court clerk does not remain open” — **Saturday and Sunday are NOT named**, exactly the structure that blocked Louisiana one gate earlier. **ONE CLAUSE SAVES IT:** 25 O.S. § 82.1(A) opens “The designation and dates of holidays in Oklahoma shall be as follows: **Each Saturday, Sunday**, …” — both weekend days, statewide, no county qualification. So the rollover reaches them and **the engine’s `isWeekend()` is correct here**, by a different textual route than any common-law state. **RECORD THIS AS A PATTERN, NOT A ONE-OFF:** three states running have had the weekend INSIDE the holiday list rather than beside it — Louisiana (Sunday only, parish-scoped Saturday → **blocked**), Oregon (Sunday in the list but Saturday also named separately in the rule → safe), Oklahoma (both, statewide → safe). **The gate question is no longer “does the rule name Saturday and Sunday” but “if it doesn’t, does the holiday statute cover both, everywhere?”** **EXCLUSION IS ELEVEN DAYS** (with TN/AZ/WI/AL), **and SIX SECTIONS ARE EXPRESSLY CARVED OUT of it** — §§ 765, 990.3, 1148.4, 1148.5, 1148.5A and 1756 count every intermediate day regardless of length. An *enumerated* opt-out, narrower than Louisiana’s open-ended “expressly excluded” and Minnesota’s open-ended opt-in, but still something a standard-level flag cannot express. None is in a first civil batch so it is recordable rather than blocking — **but seeding one later without noticing would apply an exclusion the statute removes, computing LATE**. The clerk-closure limb sits in BOTH the rollover and the exclusion and is a **partial-closure** test (“does not remain open … until the regularly scheduled closing time”), same breadth as Oregon’s; additional to the list rather than a replacement, so EARLY. **THE CALENDAR IS NOT FULLY DERIVABLE, FOR TWO INDEPENDENT REASONS.** (a) **The Christmas rule is the most complex encountered anywhere** — Dec 25, plus “the day before **or** after Christmas if Christmas is not on a Saturday or Sunday”, plus “the Thursday and Friday before Christmas if Christmas is on a Saturday”, plus “the Monday and Tuesday after Christmas, if Christmas is on a Sunday” — two or three holidays depending on the weekday, and **the general both-ways shift expressly does NOT apply to Christmas**. The before-or-after choice is simply not in the statute. (b) **§ 82.1(B) requires the Governor to issue an Executive Order EACH YEAR** specifying the dates — the **New Jersey annual-order shape layered on a statute** — and it is the only thing that resolves the Christmas choice. So Oklahoma is **ingest-per-year**, joining NJ, NC and MD, and is the first to be so for a **single day inside an otherwise derivable list**. The presidential limb (“the Governor **may** issue an Executive Order”) is discretionary and ad hoc on top: EARLY. **DO NOT ENCODE JUNETEENTH ON 19 JUNE.** Oklahoma’s is 25 O.S. **§ 82.4**, “**the third Saturday in June**”, a separate section — and § 2006 defines legal holiday by reference to **§ 82.1 only**. Practically it changes nothing because the third Saturday is already a holiday under “Each Saturday”, so it is **functionally invisible to deadline computation** — but encoding a 19 June holiday by analogy to the ten other seeded states would add a non-holiday and compute **LATE**. Also **no Columbus/Indigenous Peoples Day at all** (as in Oregon), and **the day after Thanksgiving IS statutory** (as in SC, unlike MN where it is a branch option). **SERVICE: +3 for mail, THIRD-PARTY COMMERCIAL CARRIER and electronic means** — ninth distinct answer on electronic service and the only one naming commercial carrier as its own category — period-lengthening, not Alabama’s federal after-expiry order. **BUT § 2006(D)’s proviso is a different TRIGGER, not a different amount:** where a summons and petition are served by mail, the answer runs from **the date of RECEIPT, or if refused the date of REFUSAL**, and takes **no +3**. Own row, or a caller supplying a service date computes EARLY. **AND THE ANSWER PERIOD CARRIES A PLAINTIFF’S ELECTION THE ENGINE CANNOT SEE:** § 2012(A) gives 20 days, but (A)(4) lets “the party requesting a summons to be issued … **elect** to have the answer served within **thirty-five (35) days** in lieu of the twenty” — a fact about what the requesting party chose, the same shape as Louisiana art. 1001’s 21-vs-30. **Two rows with unmissable names; guessing 35 where 20 applies computes LATE.** A defendant’s reservation of time adds a further 20 days while waiving defences — unseeable, but it only ever extends, so EARLY. **NOT DETERMINED:** the discovery periods (§§ 3233, 3234, 3236 — same source, effort remaining not a source problem); whether any of the six carved-out sections belongs in a civil batch; the current year’s Executive Order; and **25 O.S. § 82.2**, whose title suggests it bears on acts performable on holidays — § 2006 points only at § 82.1, but § 82.2 must be read to confirm it does not widen the definition **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 9 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_oklahoma.json`, new standards `ok_12_2006` and `ok_12_2006_d`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_ok_calendar.py`. `deadline-oklahoma.test.js` 51/51.** All three gate conditions met. **(1) THE TWO FACTS THE ENGINE CANNOT DERIVE ARE FOUR SEPARATE ROWS, never one with a default** — the plaintiff’s 20-vs-35 election under § 2012(A)(4), and § 2006(D)’s proviso, which on MAILED process changes the **TRIGGER** to **receipt or refusal** rather than the amount. Trigger names carry both facts (`..._20_day_election`, `receipt_or_refusal_of_mailed_...`), a bare `service_of_summons_and_petition` matches **nothing**, and the test asserts all four exist and that none carries the extension. Guessing 35 where 20 applies is **fifteen days LATE**. **(2) THE SIX CARVED-OUT SECTIONS ARE RECORDED** (§§ 765, 990.3, 1148.4, 1148.5, 1148.5A, 1756 are excluded from the 11-day exclusion); none is in this batch, and the test asserts **no seeded row is under 11 days** so none reaches it. **(3) THE CALENDAR OMITS ONE DAY A YEAR AND DISCLOSES IT.** § 82.1(A) gives “the day before **OR** after Christmas” without saying which, and § 82.1(B) makes the Governor’s annual Executive Order the only resolver. **THOSE ORDERS ARE REACHABLE AND UNREADABLE** — sos.ok.gov publishes them, identifiable by title (**EO 2026-26 AMENDED**, 15 July 2026, for calendar 2027), but each is a **single-page scanned image with zero extractable text** (`/ProcSet [/PDF /ImageI]`, one `/Im1 Do`). Reading it needs OCR, which this project does not treat as verbatim. **Omitting reports EARLY; guessing would be wrong in BOTH directions at once** — adding a non-holiday (LATE) while still missing the real one. Affected years 2026, 2028–2031; **2027 is clean** (Saturday Christmas → the Thursday and Friday before, determinate). **THRESHOLD IS ELEVEN, not seven** — the test cross-asserts Oregon’s 7 against Oklahoma’s 11 so neither can drift into the other. **NO JUNETEENTH** (§ 2006 points at § 82.1 alone; Oklahoma’s is the **third Saturday in June** under § 82.4, already a non-day), **NO COLUMBUS DAY**, and **§ 82.2 was READ** to confirm its commemorative days stay outside — all three asserted by the generator. **The day after Thanksgiving IS a statutory holiday.** § 3236 turned out to need a **`resolve: later_of`** nobody had flagged: “The thirty-day response period shall not commence until an answer to the petition is filed”, so the clock runs 30 days from the LATER of request-service and answer-filing — seeding it flat would compute EARLY. **§§ 3233/3234 have NO defendant floor** (four phrasings searched, zero hits). Extension is +3 for mail, **third-party commercial carrier** (unique to Oklahoma) and **electronic means**; e-mail is NOT named and does not qualify. **Both tests proven to catch their defects:** encoding the omitted 24 Dec 2026 fails 2 of 51 including the arithmetic case, and dropping 11 to 7 fails the cross-assertion | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Oregon gate PASSED — the Council-on-Court-Procedures wrinkle is REAL but is a CURRENCY problem, and ORCP 10 answers Maryland’s open ambiguity outright** | **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28** | unassigned | — | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-oregon-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Oregon (~4.3M). **THE COUNCIL WRINKLE, CHECKED FIRST AND CONFIRMED REAL:** the ORCP are **not promulgated by the Supreme Court**. The Council on Court Procedures drafts and promulgates them, the **Legislative Assembly may then amend or repeal**, and they take effect as law the following 1 January — a two-stage lifecycle visible in the rules’ own citation lines, which carry **two amending authorities** (“CCP 12/6/14” alongside “2015 c.212 §8”). No other seeded jurisdiction has this. **AND THE COUNCIL’S OWN SITE DISCLAIMS ITS COPY:** “the first link is **static and may not include the most recent Council amendments nor amendments by the Legislature**”. Its static file is labelled **“Effective January 1, 2020”**, its “Amendments Made By Oregon Legislature” section is **empty and pending**, and its own news page records **2024 promulgations** (Rules 1, 14, 39, 55 and a new Rule 35) the ORCP page does not reflect. **So do NOT use the Council copy as the rule text** — take it from `oregonlegislature.gov/bills_laws/SiteAssets/ORCP.html`, the whole ORCP in one ~1.1 MB HTML file, **curl 200**, which is what was read. Standing consequence: the Legislature’s file is the TEXT and the Council’s promulgation record is the **forward channel** showing what becomes law next 1 January; both must be checked, in that order — the two-channel shape Maryland has, reached for a completely different structural reason. **IT DOES NOT HAVE LOUISIANA’S PROBLEM, AND THE DRAFTING IS WHY.** ORCP 10 A rolls off **“a Saturday or a legal holiday, including Sunday”** — Saturday named SEPARATELY, Sunday folded INTO the holiday definition. So although ORS 187.010(1)(a) makes “Each Sunday” a legal holiday (the same statutory quirk Louisiana has), Oregon’s rule rolls Saturday in its own right, statewide, and **the engine’s `isWeekend()` is correct here**. Worth stating explicitly because the two statutes look superficially alike and Louisiana was blocked on exactly this. **ORCP 10 A EXPRESSLY ANSWERS THE MARYLAND OPEN DECISION:** the sub-7-day threshold applies to the period **“(without regard to section B of this rule)”** — section B being the service extension — so **the 7-day test is measured BEFORE the 3 days are added**. Maryland’s Rule 1-203 leaves that identical question unresolved and it is logged as ship-disclosed-do-not-guess. Oregon does not decide Maryland, but it proves the question is a known one, that a drafter saw it coming, and **which way that drafter resolved it** — **carry this into the Maryland decision row as persuasive material.** **OTHER FIELDS:** exclusion is **seven**; the holiday basis is an express cross-reference to **BOTH ORS 187.010 and 187.020** (most states name one); there is a public-office-closure limb **broader than any seen** — it fires when the office is closed “before the end of **or for all of** the normal work day”, so a **partial-day** closure counts — and it is ADDITIONAL to the rollover rather than a replacement, so EARLY; no backward provision; and **ORCP 10 A disapplies itself to time limitations governed by ORS 174.120**, which was NOT read and must be before seeding. **ORCP 10 B IS THE BROADEST SERVICE ENUMERATION YET** — **+3 for mail, e-mail, facsimile AND electronic service** (eighth distinct answer on electronic service), period-lengthening not Alabama’s federal after-expiry order, and **“Except for service of summons” is stated in the rule itself**, removing the single most repeated inference in this engine — every other seeded state required deducing the Rule 4/Rule 5 scope split. **ORS 187.010 HAS A SELF-REFERENTIAL TRAP:** the shift is both ways, but the Sunday limb reads “Each time a holiday, **other than Sunday**, … falls on Sunday” — **without that carve-out every Sunday would make the following Monday a holiday**, because Sunday is itself item (1)(a). A blanket Sunday-shift generator would produce **52 phantom Mondays a year**. Being both-ways it also spills across the year boundary, so compute a wider span than you emit. ORS 187.020 adds Governor-appointed days and presidential days **only when the Governor also appoints** — note the conjunctive: a presidential day alone is NOT an Oregon holiday. Ad hoc, EARLY. **NO COLUMBUS/INDIGENOUS PEOPLES DAY AT ALL**, unlike AL, SC, LA, MN, MO, NJ, VA and MA. **OREGON HAS NO WRITTEN INTERROGATORIES.** Searching the entire ORCP for “interrogator” returns **four hits, all jury interrogatories** under Rule 61 C. There is no discovery-interrogatory rule at all — **every other seeded state has an interrogatory row; Oregon must have none, and that is the law rather than unfinished work**, which the seed readme has to say or the absence reads as a gap. **PERIODS:** ORCP 7 C(2) — defendant must **“appear and defend within 30 days”** (that phrasing, not “file an answer”), or 30 days from the date stated in the summons if served by publication; ORCP 43 B production **30 days with a 45-day defendant floor**; ORCP 45 B admissions **30/45 with silence ADMITTING**, plus a mandatory capital-letters warning on the request itself, the same device Missouri’s R. 59.01(a)(1) uses. Both floors run from **service of the summons**, a caller-supplied date, so Oregon does NOT hit the Maryland chained-floor gap **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 6 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_oregon.json`, new standards `or_orcp_10` and `or_orcp_10_b`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_or_calendar.py`. `deadline-oregon.test.js` 52/52.** All four gate conditions met: text taken from the **Legislature’s** file not the Council’s static copy (asserted — every row’s URL is `oregonlegislature.gov`); **NO interrogatory row**, asserted so its absence cannot be read as unfinished work; the Sunday shift applied **only to holidays other than Sunday**, asserted by count in both the generator and the test; and **ORS 174.120 read** — it is the STATUTORY computation rule, it counts the same way but has **NO short-period exclusion at all**, so a statutory Oregon row must not use `or_orcp_10`. Not reached by any seeded row. **“EXCEPT FOR SERVICE OF SUMMONS” IS IN THE RULE’S OWN WORDS**, so the two 7 C(2) rows carry no extension and nothing rests on the inference that was got wrong federally. **THE EXTENSION REACHES E-MAIL, FAX AND ELECTRONIC SERVICE** — the opposite of NV/WV/NY/federal; the test asserts Nevada still adds 0 for e-mail on the same date Oregon adds 3, so if either ever matches the other one is wrong. **BOTH DISCOVERY FLOORS ARE `resolve_periods`** with different counts per limb (30/45), verified to pick the **45-day limb from the EARLIER trigger date** — exactly the case a plain `later_of` gets wrong — and to refuse `INCOMPLETE_TRIGGERS` on a partial set. The two floors use **different summons triggers** (43 B(2) from the summons, 45 B from the summons AND complaint) and the test asserts they stay distinct. **Test proven to catch a defect before being trusted:** injecting a phantom Columbus Day fails 3 of 52, including the arithmetic case. **The ORCP 10 A “without regard to section B” finding still belongs in the Maryland decision row** as persuasive material on the identical open question | L |

IT IS NOT AN OVERSIGHT.** `deadline-utah.test.js:232` asserts it explicitly — *"Utah declares NO coverage entry -- its gaps are all EARLY and row-level"* — so the seeding session decided the disclosure belongs on the rule rows rather than at jurisdiction level and locked that decision with a test. (2) **`legacy` returns HTTP 200 with an ERROR-PAGE BODY for a rule that does not exist** — `urcp.html` is 200/11KB of “We couldn’t find your page”. **A 200 from this host is not proof the rule exists**; every other gated source announced absence with a 403/404/paywall. **WEEKEND CHECK PASSES AT STEP 1** — first state to do so; see the standing-check row. **DAYS ARE DAYS, affirmatively:** 6(a)(1)(B) counts “every day, including intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays” → `short_period_exclusion_days` must be **ABSENT, not 0**. **BACKWARD IS EXPRESSLY DEFINED** (6(a)(5)), like Minnesota. **THE HOLIDAY LIST IS IN THE RULE (6(a)(6), thirteen limbs, “means” not “includes”) — Utah is DERIVE, not ingest**, the opposite of NC/NJ/MD/OK/CT. Utah-specific days: **Pioneer Day 24 July** and “Washington and Lincoln Day”. Not derivable from the rule ALONE though — “the day for **observing**” points at Utah Code § 63G-1-301(2), a **both-ways** Sat→preceding-Fri / Sun→following-Mon shift that **SPILLS ACROSS THE YEAR BOUNDARY**: New Year’s 2028 falls on a Saturday, so **2027-12-31 belongs in the 2027 calendar**. **⚠ THE LANDMINE:** § 63G-1-301 is **SUPERSEDED 1/1/2027** (amended TWICE in the 2026 session, Ch. 124 then Ch. 126) and the change **moves Juneteenth** out of (1)(b)(ix) into (1)(a)(ii), deleting its bespoke (2)(c)/(2)(d) Monday rules. URCP 6(a)(6)(E)’s parenthetical “the third Monday of June” is **verified EXACTLY RIGHT for the current statute in every year 2024–32** — and **wrong in 8 of the next 9 from 1/1/2027**; 2027 itself is **three days apart** (rule 06-21 vs statute 06-18). **IT FAILS BOTH WAYS IN THE SAME YEAR** — rolling off 06-21 when it is not a holiday is **LATE**. **Does NOT block seeding, blocks the calendar’s RANGE:** cap the calendar at 2026-12-31 and the engine refuses 2027+ on `missingYear` by itself, the Connecticut hold scoped to a year. **7 DAYS FOR MAIL, NOT 3** (6(c)) — copying a neighbour’s 3 computes four days EARLY; but the condition is “**exclusively** by mail under 5(b)(3)(C)(i)” and this engine has ONE `service_method` field, so a caller saying `mail` for a party also served by email gets **+7 and a LATE date**. **SETTLE BEFORE SEEDING.** 5(b)(4): service by mail or electronic means is **complete upon sending**. **REAL ANSWER DEADLINES, unlike Connecticut:** 12(a)(1) is **21 days in-state / 30 days out-of-state** (two rows, distinct triggers), plus 14 days after notice of the court’s action and 14 after a more definite statement; discovery is uniform **28 days** across 33, 34(b)(2) and 36. **EARLY-only, not modelled:** 6(a)(3) clerk inaccessibility (**additional to** the rollover — Minnesota’s shape, NOT Wisconsin’s), 6(d) unrepresented-party service-vs-filing trigger, 6(e) inmate mailbox, limb (M) + § 63G-1-301(5) governor proclamations, and **Good Friday** (a statutory holiday on a WEEKDAY that 6(a)(6) omits — omit it, that is the safe direction). **ORDER OF WORK: (1) settle “exclusively”, (2) seed with a 2026-capped calendar, (3) settle Juneteenth before extending it**. **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-27 (Hank) — 9 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_utah.json`, on Michael’s direction to seed everything not depending on the mail question.** URCP 12(a)(1) **21 in-state** and **30 out-of-state** as TWO rows with explicit `service_of_summons_in_state` / `_out_of_state` triggers (a bare `service_of_summons` matches nothing — picking wrong is a nine-day error), crossclaim 21, counterclaim 21, 12(a)(1)(A) **14 from NOTICE of the court’s action**, 12(a)(1)(B) **14 from SERVICE of the more definite statement** (not the order granting it), and 33(b) / 34(b)(2) / 36(c)(1) all **28**. `effective_from` is REAL PER RULE from each page’s own `Effective:` line — 12 → 2024-05-01, 33 → 2011-11-01, 34 → 2017-05-01, 36 → 2021-05-01. Louisiana (~4.6M), a **civil-law** jurisdiction with a Code of Civil Procedure rather than rules of court, read fresh on the assumption that none of the accumulated pattern transfers — and that paid off immediately. `legis.la.gov` publishes the C.C.P. and Revised Statutes free on plain curl with per-article Acts history, so sources are fine. **THE BLOCKER: `isWeekend()` IS WRONG FOR MOST OF LOUISIANA.** C.C.P. art. 5059(A) rolls the last day **only “unless it is a legal holiday”** — it does NOT separately name Saturday and Sunday the way every common-law rule seeded does. La. R.S. 1:55(A)(1) then makes **Sundays** a statewide legal holiday but makes **Saturday a legal holiday ONLY in enumerated parishes** — Orleans, the city of Baton Rouge, the parishes of the 2nd and 6th congressional districts except Ascension, and the parishes of the 14th and 31st judicial districts. **Everywhere else a Saturday is an ordinary day and the deadline does NOT roll.** This engine rolls BOTH weekend days unconditionally for every jurisdiction, so Louisiana deadlines landing on a Saturday would roll in the majority of parishes where the law does not roll them — a date **LATER than the true deadline**. **This is the first jurisdiction where a core engine assumption is affirmatively wrong, and it cannot be fixed in a calendar file.** It is geographic like MA’s Suffolk County and AL’s Mardi Gras, but those were SAFE — omitting a county-only extra holiday runs EARLY. Louisiana inverts it: the engine would apply a holiday that does not exist in most of the state. **A coverage disclosure cannot fix a LATE-direction error**, the standing rule since Kentucky. **THREE OPTIONS, NONE CHOSEN:** (1) seed only the Saturday-holiday parishes as their own jurisdiction code and refuse the rest — honest, covers a minority of the state; (2) add a per-jurisdiction **weekend flag** to the computation standard (default `[Sat, Sun]`, `[Sun]` for `la`) — correct, and the largest, because weekend handling is shared by all nineteen seeded jurisdictions and needs a full regression pass; (3) refuse Louisiana and record why. **ART. 5059 ALSO INVERTS THE FRAMING:** “A legal holiday is **INCLUDED** in the computation … except when (1) it is expressly excluded, (2) it would otherwise be the last day, (3) the period is less than seven days.” Exception (3) reaches the same arithmetic as the engine’s existing 7, but (1) is a **per-article opt-OUT** a standard-level flag cannot express — the mirror of Minnesota’s opt-IN. And **“A half-holiday is considered a legal holiday”** is the **exact opposite of South Carolina**, gated one day earlier, whose R. 6(a) says a half holiday is NOT a holiday — and it is not academic, because R.S. 1:55 creates real half-holidays in **Sabine and Vernon parishes**. Art. 5059(D) carries a **second computation standard inside one article** — holidays excluded outright for administrative appeals, with named carve-outs for Revenue, Environmental Quality and Insurance — which must never reach civil litigation. **STATEWIDE HOLIDAYS NO OTHER JURISDICTION HAS:** Battle of New Orleans (8 Jan), **Mardi Gras** (statewide here, where in ALABAMA it is two counties only), **Good Friday** (full statewide, where Wisconsin needed a bespoke clause and South Carolina omits it), Huey P. Long Day (30 Aug), All Saints’ Day (1 Nov). **CONDITIONAL PERIODS THE ENGINE CANNOT SEE:** art. 1001 gives **21 days to answer, but 30 if the plaintiff served a discovery request with the petition** — a fact about what the plaintiff did, so these must be **separate rows with unmissable names**, never one row with a default (guessing 30 where 21 applies runs LATE); and art. 1458 gives interrogatories 30 days **but 15 in FAMILY LAW cases** unless served with the original petition — a domain-scoped period no seeded jurisdiction has, belonging in its own domain. **No 30/45 defendant floor appears at all**, consistent with reading this jurisdiction fresh. **CURRENCY CAVEAT:** the served text is updated only through the **2025 First Extraordinary Session**, with 2026 Regular Session changes on a separate list that any seed must cross-check — the Maryland Rules-Order shape; art. 5059 already shows Acts 2025 No. 250. **NOT DETERMINED, and the first is the most important:** whether **any** C.C.P. article adds days for service by mail or electronic means — art. 1313 has none, and assuming “no extension” without confirming is exactly the inherited assumption this fresh read existed to avoid; art. 1467’s admissions deadline; art. 1462(B)(2)’s exception; and which parishes comprise the named congressional and judicial districts — noting congressional districts are **redistricted**, so that membership is not stable over time | L |

So the answer is a UNION of the rule’s eleven, the statute’s thirteen and the federal list — and the Committee Comment says so: “the net effect is the inclusion of all holidays whether state or federal”. **§ 1-3-8 adds two the rule omits — Confederate Memorial Day (4th Mon April) and Jefferson Davis’ birthday (1st Mon June)** — both still legal holidays via limb (B), so encoding only the rule’s eleven computes EARLY. **(4) THANKSGIVING HAS NO STATUTORY DATE:** § 1-3-8(a)(12) says “Thanksgiving Day, **as designated by the Governor**”, so unlike every other seeded state it is not derivable from the holiday statute and must come from the federal limb. **THE SHIFT IS THE CLEANEST YET** — § 1-3-8(b) shifts BOTH WAYS, unconditionally, for “any holiday”: no enumerated subset (contrast Minnesota’s five named days) and no “for the purposes aforesaid” caveat (contrast South Carolina). Both the Judicial Branch and the Legislature publish free on **plain curl 200**, no paywall, challenge or terms gate. **ACCESS TRAP RECORDED:** the obvious `sccourts.org/courtReg/displayRule.cfm?ruleID=6.0&ruleType=RCP` returns **HTTP 200 and 458 KB** — but the body is a NAVIGATION INDEX of every rule in every body of rules, containing none of “Computation”, “intermediate Saturdays” or “shall not be included”. **A 200 with a large payload is not proof you fetched the thing you asked for.** The working path is `/resources/judicial-community/court-rules/civil/rule-6/`. **A CORRECTION ON THE RECORD, BEFORE ANYTHING IS BUILT ON IT:** a web search returned, as Rule 6(e), that “Service by e-mail will be treated the same as service by U.S. Mail … therefore, five days shall be added”. **That sentence is not in Rule 6(e)** — the actual rule reaches service “by mail or upon a person designated by statute to accept service” and says nothing about e-mail. I then read the **2022 Supreme Court order “RE: Service by E-Mail in the Trial Courts”** (App. Case 2022-000029, 6 May 2022) end to end: paragraphs (a)–(f), and **no time-extension provision whatsoever**. So on the two sources actually read, **whether the five-day mail extension reaches e-mail is UNRESOLVED**. Direction decides the default: omitting runs EARLY (safe), adding runs LATE — **so seed mail-only and disclose the open question; do not add an e-mail extension on the strength of a search result.** Third form of the Tennessee lesson — same instinct to check the service materials, opposite outcome. **FIRST STATE REQUIRING A STATE-PLUS-FEDERAL UNION:** R. 6(a) rolls off “a Saturday, Sunday **or a State or Federal holiday**”. Every seeded state keys on one list, or on two STATE lists (Wisconsin). This matters concretely — **Juneteenth and Columbus Day are federal holidays and are NOT in S.C. Code § 53-5-10**, so encoding only the state list would miss both and compute EARLY. **FIVE DAYS FOR MAIL, NOT THREE**, and the drafting Note says so: “the additional time … is increased from 3 to 5 days.” A three-day extension copied from a neighbour computes **two days EARLY on every mailed SC period**. It also reaches service on a **statutory agent**, which no other seeded rule does. **THE STATE LIST IS LONG AND DISTINCTIVE:** Confederate Memorial Day (10 May), the day after Thanksgiving **in the statute itself** (where Minnesota leaves it to a branch option), and a **THREE-DAY Christmas block, 24, 25 and 26 December** — no other seeded jurisdiction has any of these. **TWO CAVEATS ON THE SHIFT, both for the bundled holiday question rather than new categories:** § 53-5-30 shifts both ways but says “for all of the purposes aforesaid” in a section captioned about **presentment of bills, notes and checks**, so whether it reaches COURT deadlines is arguable on the text (a smaller cousin of the Maryland list-but-not-shift problem); and it shifts only “the holidays mentioned in Section 53-5-10”, i.e. the STATE half — the federal half has its own observance rules under 5 U.S.C. § 6103, so SC may need **two shift rules for two halves of one union**, which no seeded generator does. Also: short-period exclusion is **seven**; **“A half holiday shall be considered as other days and not as a holiday”** is an express carve-out with no analogue anywhere seeded (inert today, but the rule pre-emptively refusing a category, so do not drop it); no backward provision; R. 6(d)’s **two-day** opposing-affidavit period is under seven and is the natural first row to seed because it actually exercises the exclusion; and R. 6(b) names 50(b), 52(b), 59 and 60(b) as non-extendable plus “the time for filing notice of intent to appeal is jurisdictional”. **Remaining effort, not a source problem:** Rules 12, 33, 34 and 36 not yet read — same host, same URL pattern **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 9 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_southcarolina.json`, new standards `sc_rcp_6` and `sc_rcp_6_e`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_sc_calendar.py`. `deadline-southcarolina.test.js` 48/48.** All three gate conditions met. **(1) THE STATE ∪ FEDERAL UNION IS BUILT AND ASSERTED** — first in the platform. **Juneteenth and Columbus Day are FEDERAL-only** and absent from § 53-5-10, and the test asserts both as SHAPE *and* ARITHMETIC; stripping the federal half fails **4 of 48**, including two date cases. **(2) FIVE DAYS, mail and STATUTORY AGENT only** — the latter unique among seeded rules — and **e-mail deliberately excluded**, with the whole unresolved question written into the standard’s comment and the seed readme. The search result claiming Rule 6(e) extends to e-mail **is not in the rule**, and the 2022 Supreme Court e-mail order was read end to end with **no time-extension provision at all**; adding the days would compute **LATE** if wrong, so the default omits them. **(3) BOTH § 53-5-30 CAVEATS RECORDED** — the “purposes aforesaid” scope question (the section is captioned *effect on presentment of bills, notes, and checks*), and the fact that it shifts only the STATE half; the federal half’s 5 U.S.C. § 6103(b) was **CHECKED to be identical**, recorded as a checked negative in case they diverge. **NEW FINDING THE GATE DID NOT COVER — GEORGIA’S “MAY” / “SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED” SPLIT IS HERE TOO, and it decides the row shape.** Rules 33 and 34 say a defendant **MAY** serve within 45 days — a PERMISSIVE alternative from a different event, so each gets its own **plain row** and the engine never chooses. Rule 36 says a defendant **SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED** — a mandatory floor, so it is a **`resolve_periods`** (30/45). Reading across would compute LATE on 33/34 and EARLY on 36, where silence ADMITS. **THE THREE-DAY CHRISTMAS BLOCK (24, 25, 26 Dec — unique) COLLIDES under the shift in 2026, 2027 and 2028**; the generator deduplicates and records the merge. **In 2027 Christmas Day itself is not an emitted date** — 25 Dec is a Saturday so it merges back onto the 24th, and 26 Dec moves forward to Monday the 27th. Also carried: **Confederate Memorial Day** (10 May, fixed) and **the day after Thanksgiving** in the statute itself. **NO BACKWARD ROWS** — Rule 6(a) has no backward provision, so 6(d)’s 10-day notice and 2-day affidavit periods are absent even though the two-day one is the only row that would exercise the 7-day exclusion. Rule 6(b)’s no-enlargement list is recorded for future rows | L |

Reconstructing from base-plus-amendments is the **Arizona method**, and Colorado has a specific reason it would bite: **Rule Change 2011(18) was a wholesale “TIME CALCULATION CHANGES” overhaul**, so any pre-2012 Rule 6 carries a “less than eleven days” exclusion that may no longer exist — wrong on the single most load-bearing property in the rule. **THE TENNESSEE QUESTION AGAIN, ON MUCH BETTER FACTS.** The consolidated text is on the Colorado Legal Resources public-access site reached from the General Assembly’s own link, and unlike Tennessee **it LOADS** (CRS TOC renders; a “Colorado - Local, State and Federal Court Rules” link is present). Reaching content needs an “I Agree” modal, **and I did not click it**. But read the modal: “**The Terms and Conditions do not apply to the text and numbering of the statutes, constitutional provisions, or court rules in the content of the site.** Pursuant to section 2-5-118, Colorado Revised Statutes, persons … **may publish, reprint, or otherwise distribute the statutes**”. So on its own words the terms exclude exactly the material this engine needs, state law affirmatively permits redistribution, and the site is maintained under contract with the **Committee on Legal Services of the General Assembly** — where Tennessee’s AOC expressly DISCLAIMED control and the URL 403s to everything. Four differences, all favourable; record them as different facts rather than collapsing Colorado into Tennessee. **NEXT ACTION, narrower than Tennessee’s:** decide whether clicking “I Agree” on a public-access site whose terms expressly exclude statutes and court rules is acceptable here. **Weigh a cheaper option FIRST:** the same page says OLLS “can provide electronic copies of specified portions of the Colorado Revised Statutes without charge” — whether that extends to the court rules is one email, and would sidestep the question entirely. **BANKED FROM § 24-11-101 REGARDLESS:** **FRANCES XAVIER CABRINI DAY, the first Monday in October** — Colorado replaced Columbus Day with it in 2020, no other jurisdiction here has it, so it is the natural “prove the calendar is read” day (the role Patriots’ Day played for MA and Truman Day for MO) **and Columbus Day must NOT be carried across** from NJ/VA/MA/MO, all of which count it. The statute links itself to courts in its own words — holidays are treated as Sunday “**and also for the holding of courts**” — so Colorado does not join the bundled which-list question on the statute side. **THERE IS NO WEEKEND-SHIFT PROVISION AT ALL** in § 24-11-101: it says nothing about a holiday falling on Saturday or Sunday, where every seeded state has one somewhere (Sunday-only in MA/MO/WI, both-ways in VA/WV/MD) — **and importing one by analogy is the exact mistake already caught three times**, so whatever Rule 6 says would have to carry that weight instead. And the governor/president “fasting or prayer or thanksgiving” limb is ad hoc and unknowable in advance — EARLY direction, disclosable, same shape as Va. § 1-210(F) **OLLS ASK DRAFTED 2026-08-26 AND NOT SENT — .** Michael directed pursuing the OLLS route rather than reconstructing from rule-change PDFs. Rule 6 from the Judicial Branch rule-change PDFs while waiting, which is the Arizona method and was refused there for the same reason.
