Wisconsin (~5.9M). Civil procedure is STATUTORY (chs. 801–847) like North Carolina, so all of it sits on the Legislature’s own free site and **everything needed returns HTTP 200 to plain curl** — no browser, no challenge, no paywall, no terms gate, no publisher redirect. **CURRENCY IS STATUTORILY CERTIFIED WITH AN AS-OF DATE ON EVERY PAGE:** “updated through 2025 Wis. Act 247 and through all Supreme Court Orders … in effect on August 5, 2026. Published and certified under s. 35.18.” Stronger than Missouri’s per-section version history or Maryland’s per-rule credits — nothing has to be cross-checked against a second source. **NO NEW ENGINE MECHANISM REQUIRED:** § 801.15(5) is VIRGINIA’s shape, not Missouri’s — mail +3 days, and fax/e-mail/e-filing **+1 day if the transmission is “completed between 5 p.m. and midnight”**, else +0. That is an AMOUNT that varies by clock, so it maps straight onto the existing `amount(method, ctx)` with `service_time`; the completion standard built for Missouri is NOT needed. **Fifth state, fifth answer on electronic service** (MA +3 in the time rule; TN service rule deems it mail → +3; MO +0 with a moved trigger; MD +0 and no cutoff; WI +1 on a clock). Found by the standing habit again, this time in reverse: § 801.14(2) — the service rule — has NO deeming and NO cutoff, because Wisconsin puts the cutoff in the TIME rule instead, the opposite of Missouri. **THE ONE THING TO SETTLE, AND IT IS THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION:** § 801.15(1)(b) uses TWO DIFFERENT TESTS in one subsection — the last day rolls unless it is **“a day the clerk of courts office is closed”** (a CLOSURE test, the North Carolina shape), while the carefully-composed holiday definition in (1)(a) governs ONLY the sub-11-day intermediate-day exclusion. **No seeded jurisdiction uses one basis for the rollover and another for the exclusion.** Wisconsin’s clerks of circuit court are COUNTY officers, so if any clerk is open on a listed day, encoding the list for rollover rolls **LATE**. (Closures not on the list run EARLY, which is safe.) The 1985 Law Revision Committee Note is exactly the wrong word for this purpose — the Good Friday/Dec 24/Dec 31 amendment “will **permit** clerks to close their offices at these times”, permit not require, which is why this cannot be waved through. **Three options, none chosen:** (1) treat the union list as a closure proxy and disclose the mismatch — needs checking that clerks actually close on every listed day; (2) seed only periods ≥ 11 days so the exclusion never fires, and disclose the rollover basis — reduces but does not remove the exposure; (3) refuse Wisconsin rollover entirely — safe, near-useless. **THE HOLIDAY DEFINITION IS A UNION OF THREE SOURCES** and neither list is a superset: § 801.15(1)(a) takes § 230.35(4)(a) OR § 995.20 “or both”, PLUS “a full day on Good Friday”. Only in 230.35(4)(a): **Dec 24 and Dec 31**. Only in 995.20: 3rd Mon Feb, **Juneteenth, Columbus Day, Veterans Day and TWO ELECTION DAYS**. And Good Friday is a FULL day only for time computation — 995.20 makes it just 11 a.m.–3 p.m. for worship, so encoding 995.20 alone would miss it. **Shift is SUNDAY-ONLY in both** (230.35(4)(a)(10) narrower still: only if Jan 1, Jul 4 or Dec 25 falls on Sunday) — **third state running** where carrying VA’s or WV’s both-ways shift would invent a Friday holiday and roll LATE. **County-scoped, same shape as MA’s Suffolk County:** 995.20 makes municipal election day a holiday “in every 1st class city” (Milwaukee) and lets counties of 750,000+ add holidays by ordinance — omitting runs EARLY, safe, same coverage treatment. **THE ANSWER RULE IS THE MOST BRANCH-HEAVY YET (§ 802.06(1)(a)):** 20 days default, 20 for a guardian ad litem from appointment, 20 for a cross-claim answer and for a reply, **45 for the state or a state officer/employee/agent**, and **45 “if a defendant in the action is an insurance company, or if any cause of action … is founded in TORT”**, plus 10 days after notice of the court’s action on a denied motion and 10 after service of a more definite statement. **The tort/insurer branch is the trap** — it turns on what the CLAIM IS ABOUT, not on service, which the engine cannot infer; getting it wrong reports 20 days when 45 is due (EARLY, so safe in direction but badly wrong for a user, and it is the branch a large share of civil filings actually fall in). Needs its own row with an unmissable trigger name, like Missouri’s three § 55.25(a) branches. **DISCOVERY IS ORDINARY:** §§ 804.08, 804.09 and 804.11 are all 30 days with a 45-day defendant floor, no outlier (unlike Missouri’s 45/45/60), silence ADMITS under 804.11, and every floor runs from **service of the summons and complaint — a caller-supplied date, NOT a computed one — so Wisconsin does NOT hit the Maryland chained-floor gap**. Short-period exclusion is **ELEVEN days**, matching TN and AZ, and Wisconsin explains why: a 1986 Judicial Council Note records the extension from 7 to 11 “to conform to that made in Rule 6 (a), F.R.C.P. in 1985” — **so the 7-vs-11 split across states is a VINTAGE ARTEFACT**, useful when gating the rest. **Not determined:** whether WI clerks of circuit court publish closure schedules per county or centrally (option 1 turns on it), and the § 801.18 e-filing subchapter was not read beyond 801.15(5)(b)’s reference. Secondary disclosables: the **“between 5 p.m. and midnight”** boundary at exactly 17:00 is genuinely ambiguous (Virginia’s “no later than”/“after” wording is not), and a transmission at 02:00 is NOT between 5 p.m. and midnight so adds nothing — a naive “after 5 p.m.” implementation gets that wrong | L |

Provisions s. 1-302 + s. 1-111) is wrong in BOTH directions — s. 1-302 rolls only off Sunday (no Saturday roll -> EARLY) while s. 1-111 adds Good Friday, Lincoln’s Birthday, Maryland Day and Defenders’ Day, four days that are NOT court holidays (-> LATE). Rule 1-203’s own committee note says it **supersedes s. 1-302**. Correct chain is Rule 1-203 -> Rule 1-202(l) -> **State Personnel & Pensions s. 9-201**. Ingest per year from `mdcourts.gov/administration/holidays`, joining NJ and NC as ingest-not-derive. **Shift is BOTH WAYS** (SPP s. 9-204) — first such state in three; do NOT carry MA’s or MO’s Sunday-only across. Doctrinal gap flagged: Rule 1-202(l) cites only s. 9-201 (list), not s. 9-204 (shift), and s. 9-202 confines that subtitle to the Executive Branch — resolved against six verbatim archived Judiciary calendar entries showing both directions applied, not by inference. **BUILD ITEMS:** the exclusion is “seven days or less”, and because `short_period_exclusion_days` is compared with a STRICT less-than, Maryland’s value is **8, not 7** (same trap the engine already documents for Texas); backward counting is NOT the mirror of forward (Rule 1-203(b) counts ALL intervening days and rolls the latest day BACKWARD — first jurisdiction needing a per-direction exclusion flag); and Rule 1-203(a)(2) is a second non-holiday roll trigger (clerk’s office not open or closed part of the day) needing an EARLY-direction disclosure | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **DECISION: Maryland’s <=7-day / mail-extension interaction — ship DISCLOSED, do not guess** | Open — **decision made 2026-08-26** | **Michael** | Bundled lawyer’s question | Md. Rule 1-203(c) adds three days **“to the prescribed period”**, so a mailed 7-day period arguably becomes a 10-day period — which is “more than seven days” — which would **flip intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and holidays from EXCLUDED to COUNTED**, changing the date. **Two live readings, no committee note, no cross reference, and no controlling authority found.** (A) “the period of time allowed” in 1-203(a) means the period AFTER the extension -> exclusion lost -> EARLIER date. (B) it means the underlying rule’s period -> exclusion survives and the 3 days are appended -> LATER date. They diverge on any mailed period of **4–7 days**. **This cannot be resolved by picking the safe side**, because which reading is safe depends on the period — that is what distinguishes it from every other disclosed gap in this engine, all of which fail consistently EARLY. **Michael’s direction 2026-08-26: ship as a disclosed ambiguity rather than guessing.** If a mailed short period is ever seeded, the engine should REFUSE rather than pick — the same call already made for W. Va. R. 6(e), which returns `refused_contested_standard` rather than resolving a rule against itself. Belongs with the bundled lawyer’s question **PERSUASIVE MATERIAL ADDED 2026-08-26 from the Oregon gate — a drafter who saw this exact question coming and answered it.** **ORCP 10 A** applies its sub-7-day threshold to the period **“(without regard to section B of this rule)”**, section B being the 3-day service extension. So Oregon measures the 7-day test on the UNDERLYING period, BEFORE the extension is added — reading (B) in the Maryland framing. That does not decide Maryland (different state, different rule) but it establishes three things worth having when someone answers this: the ambiguity is a KNOWN one rather than a quirk of Maryland’s drafting, at least one drafter anticipated it, and the resolution that drafter chose was the one that PRESERVES the exclusion. Md. Rule 1-203 has no equivalent parenthetical, which is precisely why it is ambiguous — so the contrast is itself the argument that Maryland’s silence should not be read as implying either answer. | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **ENGINE GAP: a discovery floor that CHAINS OFF A COMPUTED DATE — highest-stakes shape found in any gate** | Open — **own row on Michael’s direction 2026-08-26** | unassigned | A design decision on rule chaining | **This is an engine gap, not a Maryland quirk.** All three Maryland discovery rules use one construction; Rule 2-424(b) verbatim: “Each matter of which an admission is requested shall be **deemed admitted** unless, within 30 days after service of the request **or within 15 days after the date on which that party’s initial pleading or motion is required, whichever is later**…”. **Why this is unlike every later-of row already seeded:** Ohio, Georgia, NJ, NC, WA, VA, MA and MO all measure a defendant floor from a date the CALLER SUPPLIES (service of the complaint, an appearance, service of process). Maryland’s second limb runs from “the date on which that party’s initial pleading or motion is **required**” — **the output of another rule** — and it is not a static offset: Rule 2-321 sets the answer at **30, 60 or 90 days** depending on how and where the defendant was served, and Rule 2-321(c) extends it further to “15 days after entry of the court’s order” on a Rule 2-322 motion or remand. So the floor is 45 / 75 / 105 days, or a date that depends on a ruling. **`resolve_periods` cannot express it** — its limbs take an event and a count, and neither limb can be “the result of computing rule X”. **Nothing seeded today handles a trigger that depends on another rule’s outcome.** **THE STAKES ARE WHY THIS IS ITS OWN ROW:** Rule 2-424 is self-executing — miss it and facts are **DEEMED ADMITTED** — so a floor guessed short computes **EARLY on the one rule where an early date silently forfeits the case on the merits**. Most dangerous single shape encountered in any gate so far. **Three options, none chosen:** (1) caller supplies the computed date via a named limb event — honest and buildable today, but a caller picking the wrong 30/60/90 branch produces an EARLY floor; (2) build real rule chaining so a limb can name another rule — correct, and much the largest; (3) refuse the defendant limb and seed only the plain 30-day row with an explicit disclosure that a defendant’s true deadline may be later — safe (EARLY), least useful. **Maryland should not be seeded piecemeal until this is decided** | L |

Rules 5.02, 6.01, 6.05, 12.01, 33.01, 34.02, 36.01 read verbatim. **WHAT FAILS IT IS ONE STATUTE.** Rule 6.01 does not define "legal holiday"; it expressly delegates to **Tenn. Code Ann. § 15-1-101**, so no statute means no calendar and every TN row refuses. **THE AOC'S OWN CODE PAGE IS THE DECISIVE EVIDENCE** (`tncourts.gov/Tennessee%20Code`, read verbatim): the LEXIS site "is not operated by, nor is it under the control of, The Tennessee Administrative Office of the Courts", and "**Lexis has changed its free version of the Tennessee Code. Before you can view the content, you must click a button that says you agree to its terms and conditions.**" **CORRECTION ON THE RECORD:** this was first relayed as the courts flagging the free edition as *unreliable*. The page does not say that — it says the AOC does not control the host and that content now sits behind a **terms-acceptance click**. The verdict is unchanged and rests on firmer ground read literally: **an agent must not click an "I agree" button on anyone's behalf** (the same line already drawn for BAILII/AustLII and with CanLII v. Caseway AI in mind), and that line is reached before any content-quality question. `lexisnexis.com/hottopics/tncode` 403s to curl AND a real browser in any case, and `advance.lexis.com` renders "Page Unavailable". Acts ch. 337 eff. 2023-05-05. **AND ONE GAP RUNS LATE, which forecloses a Virginia-style disclosure:** § 15-1-101 grants all election days as legal holidays on its face while its own Compiler's Notes record Acts 1984 ch. 979 letting the governor DELETE holidays and his choosing the election-day ones. **DISTINCT FROM BOTH PRIOR REFUSALS, do not collapse them:** Kentucky has no free rules at all plus a holiday basis failing LATE; Arizona was a *completeness/staleness* problem later cleared on `govt.westlaw.com`'s free government edition — **that clearance does NOT carry here**, because reading a page that simply loads is not the same act as accepting a contract. **What would reopen it:** a human obtaining § 15-1-101 through the gate and supplying the verbatim current text plus the governor's standing designations under Acts 1984 ch. 979. | **SAIRNlaw** | **Banked from the Tennessee gate — three findings that would corrupt a seed if carried across from a neighbour** | Open (reference, no action) | unassigned | — | Kept as its own row because these are reusable regardless of whether Tennessee is ever seeded, and two of them are traps this project has already hit once in another form. **(1) THE SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION IS ELEVEN DAYS, NOT SEVEN.** Tenn. R. Civ. P. 6.01: "When the period of time prescribed or allowed is less than eleven (11) days, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays shall be excluded." Every other seeded state with an exclusion uses 7 (NJ, NC, WA, MA, WV appellate); only Arizona also uses 11. Encoding 7 by analogy would be wrong on every TN period of 7–10 days. Rule 6.04's five-day motion notice is the row that would actually exercise it — seed that one first if TN ever clears. **(2) A TIME RULE THAT READS MAIL-ONLY MAY NOT BE.** Tenn. R. Civ. P. 6.05 adds three days for service "by mail" and says nothing about electronic service — but **Rule 5.02(2)(c)** deems an emailed document "a document that was mailed for purposes of computation of time under Rule 6", and **Rule 5.02(3)(e)** does the same for E-service. So email and e-service DO get the three days, via the SERVICE rule rather than the time rule, and reading 6.05 alone gives a wrong EARLY answer. Facsimile has no such deeming provision and gets nothing. Tennessee reaches Massachusetts' outcome by a completely different route. **Standing habit this should become: check the service rule for deeming provisions before concluding a time rule is mail-only.** **(3) THE CLEANEST STATEMENT YET OF THE BUNDLED HOLIDAY QUESTION, AND IT POINTS AT THE STATUTE.** Advisory Commission Comment [2011] to Rule 6.01: "The status of a day as a legal holiday is statutory; thus, for the purpose of filing papers in court, **it does not depend on whether the clerk's office is open for business** … state offices might be open on Columbus Day … in such circumstances, however, Columbus Day is still a 'legal holiday' for purposes of computing time periods under the rule." The **exact inverse of North Carolina**, whose Rule 6(a) keys on courthouse closure — and it means the TN state-employee holiday schedule (§ 4-4-105) is the WRONG source, in terms. Worth carrying into the bundled lawyer's question as the model answer a rule can give. Also banked: TN Rules 33.01, 34.02 AND 36.01 all carry a 30-day period with a 45-day defendant floor, so all three need `later_of` rows (Massachusetts has it on only two of three); § 15-1-101's weekend shift runs BOTH ways like VA and WV, unlike MA's Sunday-only; and Good Friday is a TN legal holiday, as in NJ | S |

What detects it is an INCOMPLETE limb set: correct placement refuses on the DOCUMENT, misplaced refuses `INCOMPLETE_TRIGGERS`. **And the separate coverage gap is closed:** these four rows were live with ZERO assertions, and `deadline-cross-appeal.test.js` now carries 86 including the `later_of` arithmetic on both limbs of all four rows, both mechanisms, each limb winning in turn. A per-limb declaration is its own design question | unassigned | — | Found seeding New York 2026-08-24; flagged in the seed readmes but it is not a New York problem. `computeDeadline()` never validates what a trigger date means — it counts from whatever it is handed. **Sharpest instance: CPLR 308.** Both `ny-cplr-320a-appearance-30-service-complete` and `ny-cplr-3012c-answer-30-service-complete` run 30 days from when *"service is complete"*, and for CPLR 308(2) leave-and-mail and 308(4) affix-and-mail that is a computed date — a fixed number of days after proof of service is **filed**, often more than a week after delivery. CPLR 308 was deliberately not read verbatim for that seed, so no completion arithmetic is encoded and none is guessed. **A user supplying the delivery date gets a deadline that is too early; supplying the filing date gets one that is too early by a different amount. Neither draws a refusal.** The same class already spans the engine: Florida's `rendition` (a term of art, not the signature or mailing date), California's Rule 8.104 service-of-notice-of-entry limbs, Texas's `signing of the judgment`, New York's `service ... with written notice of its entry`. Four jurisdictions now start an appeal clock four different ways and nothing stops a caller conflating them. **West Virginia makes a fifth (2026-08-25):** R. App. P. 5(b) runs from `entry of the final order OR MANDATE`, and 5(f) from `the date the judgment ... was entered` — so within one state the notice-of-appeal clock and the perfect-the-appeal clock can start from two different documents, and neither is derived. Next action: decide between (a) encoding CPLR 308's completion arithmetic as a real computation so the engine derives it, and (b) a `trigger_requires_derivation` flag on such rows that makes the endpoint surface a named warning alongside the date rather than returning a bare one. The Slabs precedent says delete the fallback and show an honest empty state; doing that to 30 panels removes the demo from the product. **Recorded with the numbers so the call is made on numbers rather than on "~28 unaudited".** **NOT DONE and not claimed:** the follow-on's second half — whether the other fifteen apps carry the same shape | L |
| **StoneDesk** | ~~Procedural stone texture likely inflates `canvas.toDataURL()` PNG snapshots against an existing quota risk~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `bf490b6d` on Michael's decision — JPEG q0.70 at full resolution, cap 50 → 20, live-verified end to end | Cody | — | **Measured in a real browser against the deployed app, not estimated.** `ct-canvas` is 640×480; `sdDrawSave()` stores `toDataURL('image/png')` in `sd_drawings`, capped at **50 entries**. Flat placeholder (no stone selected): **18.4 KB PNG / 25,114 chars**. Worst realistic textured case (U-shape, granite/dark): **49.9 KB PNG / 68,186 chars** — a **2.7×** inflation, so the premise holds. All twelve stone/tone combinations measured; granite is the most expensive by a wide margin (45–46 KB) and quartzite/light the cheapest (18.1 KB, indistinguishable from flat).<br><br>**THE CONSEQUENCE IS PLATFORM-WIDE, WHICH THIS ROW DID NOT ANTICIPATE.** localStorage counts UTF-16, so one worst-case entry costs **133 KB of quota**, and **50 × 133 KB ≈ 6.5 MB against a ~5 MB origin budget**. The 50-entry cap is therefore not a bound that protects anything. And `sairn.vercel.app` is **one origin shared by every SAIRN app** — measured live: **199 keys, 1.25 MB already in use** before a single drawing exists, `sd_slabs` alone 595,280 chars. A rep filling StoneDesk's drawing history does not degrade StoneDesk; it **starves every other SAIRN app on the same origin**, and their `st()` failures would surface as their own "could not save" messages, blamed on the wrong app.<br><br>**WHAT IS ALREADY FINE, checked rather than assumed:** the write is honest — `st()` returns false and `sdDrawSave()` already shows *"Could not save — storage may be full or unavailable"*, so this fails loudly, not silently. And `sd_drawings` is **local-only**: it appears in no resource registry and no sync list, so the 64 KB `MAX_PAYLOAD_BYTES` cap in `api/sd-data.js` is not in play. Both hypotheses were checked and both came back clean.<br><br>**THE DECISION, with measured options rather than opinions — all four numbers are quota cost per entry, worst case:** today PNG 640×480 = **133 KB**; JPEG q0.85 same size = **70 KB**; JPEG q0.70 = **49 KB**; PNG at half scale (320×240) = **58 KB**; JPEG q0.85 at half scale = **23.5 KB**. Lowering the 50-entry cap is the other lever and costs no fidelity. **Not changed, because every option trades something a customer sees** — JPEG blurs crisp line art, downscaling loses detail on a drawing an operator reads dimensions from, and a smaller cap silently shortens history. This row asked to measure before changing anything; the measurement was done first and the change followed.<br><br>**DECIDED AND SHIPPED 2026-09-04 (Michael's call): JPEG q0.70 at FULL resolution, plus the cap 50 → 20.** Downscaling was rejected deliberately — an operator reading a dimension off an old drawing is a **functional** risk, not a cosmetic one.<br><br>**THE VISUAL CHECK BEFORE SHIPPING CAUGHT WHAT THE ARITHMETIC COULD NOT, and it would have been a correctness regression.** `ct-canvas` is **transparent** — the pale field is CSS, not pixels — and JPEG has no alpha, so a bare `toDataURL('image/jpeg', 0.70)` **flattens it to BLACK**. Seen in the browser: the grid lines vanish entirely, dark grey on black, and the drawing comes back as something the rep never drew. Shipping on the numbers alone would have traded a size problem for a worse one. The snapshot is now composited onto the canvas's **own computed** background first, read from `getComputedStyle` rather than hardcoded, with a white fallback because falling back to *none* is the black field again.<br><br>**Result, measured on the deployed build by driving the real `sdDrawSave()`:** 68,186 chars → **29,123 chars** (0.43×), quota cost **133 KB → 57 KB** per entry, whole store **~6.5 MB → ~1.1 MB**. Side by side the edge colour-coding, the texture and the panel label are all legible, and the JPEG is if anything closer to what the rep sees on screen than the transparent PNG was. **The live origin was restored exactly** — `sd_drawings` removed again, 654,296 chars and 199 keys before and after. 15 assertions in `tests/stonedesk_drawing_snapshot_budget.js`, four mutations probed; the composite has one assertion per load-bearing part, because the fill must happen BEFORE the `drawImage` or it covers the drawing.<br><br>**~~FOUND WHILE HERE, NOT FIXED — `sd_drawings` is WRITTEN AND NEVER READ.~~ CLOSED the same day (Cody) in `726677f9` on Michael's decision to build the missing half rather than delete the feature.** A **Saved Drawings** list now sits on the Drawing Tool toolbar: each row shows the drawing's own snapshot, its name and date, and loads it back onto the canvas. **The decision that made it more than a list is a correctness one:** the store held a PICTURE and nothing else, so a "reload" could only have painted a raster onto a canvas whose model still described something else — shape, dimensions and cutouts would all be whatever was on screen, and the first recalculation would contradict the image. `sdDrawSave()` therefore also stores `dcSnapshotDrawingState()`, **the same snapshot the quote history already keeps**, and the loader is the same `dcLoadDrawingState()` with the same `schemaVersion` check — no second mechanism. **Entries saved before this are listed, greyed, and told why** ("saved before drawings kept their measurements"), because hiding them reads as data loss and enabling them would be a control that does nothing. The unsaved-work confirm moved into **one** `dcHasUnsavedWork()` used by both loaders, with a test asserting there is exactly one definition. **Deletion deliberately not added** — a list and a reload is what was asked for, the 20-entry cap already rolls entries off, and an unrequested destructive control is not mine to add. 32 assertions, four mutations; live-verified on the deployed build and the rendered panel photographed.<br><br>**`SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`'s 2026-08-13 entry is now partly STALE and is corrected here rather than left to mislead:** it predicted a *"5-15x"* inflation (measured: **2.7×**) and described `sdDrawSave()` as swallowing quota failures silently while claiming success. **That half was fixed at some point since** — `st()` returns a boolean the caller reads and the status line says *"Could not save"* on a refused write, both asserted so they cannot regress | M |

| **SAIRNbiz** | ~~No server-side persistence for anything except the employee roster &mdash; and the ledger implies more data integrity than actually exists~~ | **CODE CLOSED 2026-09-04 (CC) &mdash; ⚠ SQL PENDING, so the gap is NOT yet closed for a customer.** THE SCOPE, MEASURED OFF THE FILE RATHER THAN TAKEN FROM THIS ROW: `sairnbiz.html` writes **SEVENTEEN** localStorage collections through `st()`/`ld()`, and exactly ONE reached a server (`sb_emps`, via the bespoke `employees` branch). **NINE are now backed up** through one generic read/write pair in `api/sd-data.js` (`SB_RESOURCES`) plus `sql/sairnbiz_data_schema.sql` &mdash; 9 tables, which **must be run in Supabase before any of it does anything**. Until then writes answer 503 NOT_PROVISIONED naming the file and reads answer `provisioned:false`, and **nothing in the UI claims a backup exists**, deliberately. **Eight collections excluded on purpose, each with its reason in the registry** &mdash; `sb_emps` already syncs through the role-gated `employees` branch (folding it in would route the roster past that gate and into a differently-tenanted second copy), `sb_co`/`sb_cfg` are single objects not keyed collections, `sb_incidents` has no write path at all, and `sb_lic`/`sb_role`/`sb_seeded`/`sb_sync` are app state. **TWO DELIBERATE DIVERGENCES FROM THE SAIRNbuild PRECEDENT, both stated rather than drifted into.** (1) **A SESSION GATE.** `BLD_RESOURCES` gates on the licence alone and argues that correctly &mdash; those are the shared job record. These nine are payroll history, performance scores and PIP flags, and the licence key is a string `sairnbiz.html` itself documents as NOT auth. It is a SESSION gate, not a ROLE gate: accounting legitimately reads AP/AR and HR legitimately reads training. (2) **ID MINTING.** Four of the nine (`sb_exps`, `sb_perf`, `sb_hire`, `sb_bud`) store records with **no identifier of any kind** &mdash; copying the precedent verbatim would have silently backed up nothing for four of nine while the other five worked, the half-applied failure the `st()` hook exists to prevent, reintroduced through the data. Ids are minted and persisted, same derive-then-persist shape `sbNormalizeBills()` already uses here. The other five are NOT minted: their id is user-visible (an invoice number, a vendor code), so a missing one is reported instead of invented. **THE SILENT-CATCH HALF, WHICH IS THE SHARPER FINDING.** `st()` was `try{...}catch(e){}` and returned NOTHING, so every caller &mdash; `saveExp`, `saveBill`, `runPayroll`, `saveCertRenewal` &mdash; showed its success toast unconditionally on the next line. **A browser that could not store the record told the user it had**, and the LEDGER half of the same action still reached Postgres, so the journal entry survived while the record it was derived from did not. It now returns a boolean and says **"THIS DID NOT SAVE"**, once per key so a render-path failure cannot toast on every repaint. **AND THE LOGIN ORDER CHANGED, because the fix would otherwise have created a new defect:** a fresh device seeded Pinnacle demo rows and THEN merged the real backup, mixing sample invoices into a real company's books. A never-seeded device now hydrates first (it has nothing to paint, so nothing is delayed) and marks itself seeded if records came back; a device that already holds data still paints first. The blocking path is bounded at 6s so an unreachable server cannot hang the app. **It is a BACKUP, not two-way sync:** per-record last-write-wins, deletions do not propagate (`api/sd-data.js` has no delete path outside `sc_*`, platform-wide). **Nothing here is a ledger defect** &mdash; `sbGlPost()` already refuses to claim a post it did not get. | CC | ⚠ Michael: run `sql/sairnbiz_data_schema.sql` | 41 assertions + **6 mutation probes on the sync and 2 on the failure voice, all 8 bite**; registry/handler/client/SQL cross-checked in all four directions (9 declared, 9 handled, 9 synced, 9 tables); 268 resources platform-wide with zero cross-app collisions | L |

Its `blId` fell back to a minted `BILL-...` whenever the invoice number was blank and that string was never stored, **while the `bill_paid` entry for the SAME bill used `b.id`** &mdash; two entries for one bill, two different source ids, neither linkable to the other. Both now name the same record; the invoice number stays on the record as the human handle but is not the key, because it is optional and two vendors can issue the same one. **The id is minted at creation and deliberately NOT left to `sbEnsureIds()`**, which also mints an `EX` id: that helper runs inside the backup hook behind a licence check and two availability flags, so on an unlicensed or offline device it never runs &mdash; and offline is exactly when the local ledger is the only copy. Both sites reuse `sbEnsureIds`/`sbNormalizeBills` formats and collision loops, so the schemes cannot diverge and two saves in the same millisecond cannot share an id. Original finding follows.  `saveExp()` builds `exId='EX-'+String(Date.now()).slice(-6)` purely to fill `source_id` on the `sbGlPost()` call, and **never writes it onto the expense record**. | **Tooling** | ~~Push-gate check 6 (the credential scan) reported itself UNCHECKED every time a push lost a race with another clone~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-04 (CC)** &mdash; root cause read out of the reflog, not reasoned; held by `tests/push_gate/redaction_base_probe.py` | CC | &mdash; | git hands a PRE-PUSH hook the sha the **remote has right now**, which by definition can be a commit this clone has never fetched. `git log <that sha>..<tip>` exits **128 Invalid revision range**, check 6 turned that into a bare RuntimeError, and the fail-open handler printed *"this push is UNCHECKED for credentials"* &mdash; so the push shipped scanned by nothing. **With four clones pushing concurrently this is the ORDINARY case, not an edge one:** it fires every time you lose a race. Observed live while pushing the SAIRNbiz work, then confirmed from `git reflog show origin/main` &mdash; the hook was handed `55a408a5` while this clone's origin/main was still `ae89eea5`, and `55a408a5` arrived on the NEXT fetch, after that push. **The fix falls back to a base this clone actually holds** (`@{u}`, then `origin/main`), which WIDENS the range &mdash; it can include commits already on origin &mdash; so the scan does more work, never less, and cannot miss a commit being sent. Fetching from inside a pre-push hook was rejected: slow, surprising, and it would make the gate depend on the network it exists to keep secrets off. It **says out loud** when it used a fallback base, because a scan over a range that is not exactly what ships must not read as one that was. The failure message now carries **git's own stderr** &mdash; the version that said only "failed" is what cost the diagnosis. **The sibling checks 1/2/3 were NOT affected, checked rather than assumed:** they read the same base through `outgoing_files()`, whose `git()` helper returns `''` on a non-zero exit instead of raising, so they already fell through. That is correct BY ACCIDENT and one edit away from being undone &mdash; making `git()` raise would silence every check on a raced push **with no message at all**, strictly worse than what was just fixed. The probe asserts it so that edit fails loudly. **Third wrong implementation of this check, and the first to fail in the ordinary case** rather than a contrived one; all three were found by driving it and missed by reasoning about it. | S |

Traced by hand, as the row asked.** **THE COLLISION IS BENIGN AND WAS DESIGNED THAT WAY.** The three `rec` writers — `saveBookingSettings()`, `dnPersistWindows()`, `saveGfeIdentity()` — are one shape, not three: each does `Object.assign({}, <base>, {only its own keys})`, so no two write a different schema into the key. `dntSyncFromServer()` writes `serverSettings`, the server row, replacing wholesale, which all three rely on and say so in comments. Entered in `tools/key_collision_check.py`'s ACKNOWLEDGED map with that reason and the exact variable pair, so a THIRD writer or a rename re-reports it. `key_collision_check.py sairndental.html` is now **CLEAN**. **THE REAL DEFECT WAS THE MERGE BASE**, which is not a collision — the same distinction the `stonedesk:ai_memories` entry already records for its own key. The base was `settings()`, **this device's cached copy**, and `api/sd-data.js` stores `data: payload`, replacing the whole blob. So: workstation B saves an appeal window (server has `[X, Y]`); workstation A, last synced before that, saves the practice identity, merges onto its stale copy, sends `[X]`, and **Y is erased on the server** — after which B's next sync loses it. This row is explicitly practice-wide configuration a second workstation must see; the per-device merge protected panel-vs-panel and nothing else. **The base is now a fresh SERVER read**, and a read that FAILS returns null so the caller refuses rather than saving on top of a base it could not check. A device with no licence key keeps its local-only save — it is not talking to a server, so there is nothing to clobber. **AND A SECOND LIVE DEFECT IN THE SAME FUNCTION:** `saveBookingSettings()` wrote to localStorage BEFORE the request, and its 409 `SLUG_TAKEN` branch only toasted and returned — so the device kept a booking slug the server had **refused**, and the form and the patient-feedback link preview advertised a slug belonging to **another practice**. All three panels now write server-first through one shared writer, which also means the other two handle `SLUG_TAKEN` instead of reporting it as a generic failure. The stale *"server sync not yet enabled for this app"* message — on every failure path of all three — is gone; sync is enabled and that string hid the real reason. **21 assertions in `tests/sairndental_settings_merge_base.js`, driving the two extracted helpers rather than reimplementing them, and three mutation probes: reverting the base to `settings()`, moving the local write back before the server call, and treating a failed read as empty. All three bite; the file was restored and verified byte-identical.** Per `sairn-code-scrubber` item 16 Shape B, each call site asserts the OLD mechanism is ABSENT as well as the new one present. | S |

It comes from the MERGED record, with its own test and its own mutation probe. **The response is the merged record too** — the client caches it, and handing back the patch would leave the device missing every key that panel does not own. **FAIL-CLOSED on an unreadable current row** (503 `SETTINGS_READ_UNAVAILABLE`), same standard as the GFE check and the coverage-rule uniqueness read; 404/400 is not a failure and falls through to `NOT_PROVISIONED`. **The client-side fresh-read base added hours earlier the same day is REMOVED, superseded rather than abandoned** — the server merging is one round trip instead of two with no window between read and write. Its test file was renamed and rewritten rather than deleted quietly. **26 assertions across the two suites, 5 mutation probes, all biting:** remove the server merge, take `booking_slug` from the patch, treat an unreadable row as empty, send the whole record from a panel, cache the patch instead of the server record. Both files verified **byte-identical by sha256** after the probe run. **The trade, as decided:** a key can no longer be REMOVED from the record, only overwritten. Cost is currently zero — nothing deletes a settings key — and a real delete need later is a small separate addition. | M |

The Cost Code Roll-up hardcodes its percentage to 0 when there is no budget, so a cost code with $5,000 committed against no budget rendered **"$5,000 / $0 · 0%" with a zero-width bar in critical red** — the colour and the number, same row, saying opposite things. Fixed: it now says *"no budget set"* and fills the bar. The roll-up **has no function of its own** — it is rendered at the end of `rCostTbl()` — which is why a change to the tier function reached it without anyone opening a file named after it. **All five of f072765's corrections were verified, four of them by mutation** against the real file: reverting the active-jobs iteration, the zero-budget tier, the roll-up percentage or the full-cost-list argument each fails the assertion that names it. **RAISED FOR A DECISION, THEN FIXED TOGETHER on Michael's call in `c7681508`** — the two were one root cause, `actual` being dropped, wearing two faces: (1) the roll-up computed its tier from **committed only** (`actual:0`) while `costLineTier` and every other panel use committed+actual, so the same data could read green in the roll-up and critical on its own row — exactly what f072765's *"unified tier colours across panels"* claim says cannot happen; (2) the **Variance** column was budget−committed and ignored `actual` entirely, so a line with budget 100 / committed 0 / actual 100 showed a healthy **+100** in the cell directly beside a **Critical** badge (pre-dates f072765) — it now reads 0. **FOUR sites carried the old arithmetic, not the two the review named:** the panel KPI, the per-row cell, the roll-up, and **the CSV export** — the last of which takes the number off the screen into a customer's spreadsheet, where no badge sits beside it to contradict. Found by grepping for the arithmetic rather than for the panels the review had named. **The change makes a reported number WORSE wherever `actual` is non-zero, so it is deliberately not silent:** the KPI sub-label reads *"Budget less committed+actual"* in both states and the export column was **renamed** to *"Variance (budget less committed+actual)"* rather than quietly recomputed — it holds a different quantity than it did. Live-verified in both directions: six new markers present on the deployed page and both old-basis markers absent. **One of my own first-draft assertions was wrong and is recorded rather than quietly corrected:** I expected 500 committed + 400 actual against a 1000 budget to be *warning*; it is 90%, so critical, and the code was right. Held by `tests/sairnbuild_budget_early_warning.js`, 30 assertions | S |

(2) Decide whether any app should ship a guard that makes this unreachable — the Access panel added in `ce8193e` would have PREVENTED it by surfacing the server's `LAST_OWNER` refusal, but nothing stops the same state being created through the API directly<br><br>**NOT A ONE-OFF — CONFIRMED AS A PATTERN ACROSS FOUR OF THE FIVE APPS, 2026-08-28.** CC raised this while auditing tonight's auth builds and I verified it independently against the source rather than accepting it: `PROVISIONING_ROLES` is a SINGLE role in **SAIRNdental** `['owner']`, **SAIRNmechanical** `['owner']`, **SAIRNroofing** `['owner']` and **SAIRNcode** `['admin']`. Only **StoneDesk** has two, `['owner','admin']`. For all four single-role apps the `setup` gate is bound to that *same* role, so there is genuinely no second path back — RF-PINNACLE-2026 is simply the one where it has already fired. **ONE REFINEMENT TO CC'S FRAMING, worth stating precisely: StoneDesk's exposure is REDUCED, not absent.** Two provisioning roles mean an `admin` can restore a deactivated `owner` and vice versa, so it takes two deactivations rather than one — but if the last active `owner` AND the last active `admin` both go inactive, StoneDesk is locked out identically. The guard question is therefore "how many provisioning-role holders must remain active", not "does this app have two roles". **ONE MORE THING THE COUNT HIDES, verified from source 2026-08-28 (Hank): THE EXISTING GUARD IS PRESENT IN ALL FIVE AND IS PREVENTIVE-ONLY.** Every one of `dnt`, `mech`, `rf`, `sc` and `sd` already carries the last-one refusal — an `activeOwners`/`activeAdmins` `.length <= 1` check before allowing a deactivation. So the exposure is **not a missing guard, it is a missing RECOVERY PATH**: the refusal stops the last active provisioner being deactivated *through* `set_active`, and can do nothing about one that is already inactive — which is exactly how RF-PINNACLE-2026 got here, via verification passes that left rows `active:false` rather than via a refused call. **Consequence for the fix: a harder guard on the deactivation path would not have prevented this and will not prevent the next one.** The two halves are separable and only the second is unsolved — widening the pool (StoneDesk demonstrates it) versus providing a way back (nothing demonstrates it). **AND THE CONCRETE TRAP FOR WHOEVER BUILDS IT: SAIRNcode’s single role is `admin`, not `owner`.** Four of the five say `owner`; `sc-auth.js` says `admin`. A recovery path that names a role instead of reading `PROVISIONING_ROLES` misses SAIRNcode **silently** — the app answers normally right up until someone needs to recover. Scope note: exactly **5 of 16** auth files implement `set_active` at all; the other eleven have neither it nor a `PROVISIONING_ROLES` constant, so they are out of scope rather than unaudited. **This is the input to the harder-guard decision** (action 2 above), and it changes its scope: a fix aimed at one app would leave three with the same exposure. Candidate shapes, none chosen: refuse the deactivation that would take active provisioning-role holders to zero (the `LAST_OWNER` guard already exists but is unreachable by construction — see `sairn-employee-auth-scaffold` §7); or add a break-glass path; or widen `PROVISIONING_ROLES` per app, which is a real authorisation change and not a safety patch | M |
| **SAIRNroofing** | `set_active` and `set_certifications` parse a PATCH body their own helper never asked for — probable false 502 AFTER a successful write | **CLOSED 2026-08-28 — fixed by another session in `05bc516`, and independently re-confirmed live by me.** The row was raised 2026-08-27 as NOT live-verified ⚠️ | unassigned | — | **Code-shape finding with a probable runtime consequence, reported as that and not as a confirmed live failure.** `patchEmployee()` at `api/rf-auth.js:174-178` sends `headers` (`api/rf-auth.js:161` — apikey, Authorization, Content-Type) with **no `Prefer` header**. PostgREST answers a PATCH without `Prefer: return=representation` with **204 No Content and an empty body**. Both call sites then parse unconditionally: `const patched = await patchR.json();` at `api/rf-auth.js:482` (`set_active`) and `:393` (`set_certifications`). `.json()` on an empty body rejects, the outer catch at `:498-506` turns it into a 502 `'Upstream error — try again'` — **after the deactivation has already been written**. That is a silent-failure inversion: the action succeeds and reports failure, so an owner deactivating someone sees an error and may retry or assume it did not work. Contrast `api/sc-auth.js:407-411` and `api/sd-auth.js:401-405`, which explicitly set `Prefer: 'return=representation'`, and `api/leg-auth.js:297` / `api/law-auth.js:399,424,439`, which only parse on the failure path. **`recordFailure`/`clearFailures` use the same helper and are unaffected** — they never call `.json()`. **Next action: reproduce live against `sairn.vercel.app` before fixing** — the fix is one header or one `if (!patchR.ok)` guard, but the whole point of this row is that nobody has actually seen it fail yet. Not folded into the skill work, hence a row<br><br>**RESOLVED, and the resolution came from two directions.** `05bc516` ("set_active returned 502 on a mutation that had already landed") applied exactly the predicted fix at BOTH call sites — `api/rf-auth.js:489` for `set_active` and `:394` for `set_certifications` — parsing the PATCH body only on the error path (`if (!patchR.ok) { ... }`) rather than unconditionally, and its comment records it as proven live 2026-08-27 with both deactivate and reactivate returning 502 while the row changed correctly underneath. **That is the failure mode this row predicted from code shape alone and explicitly declined to assert without reproduction — the caution was right and so was the diagnosis.** Independently re-confirmed 2026-08-28 during the Access-panel round trip: eight consecutive `set_active` calls (deactivate, idempotent repeat, second-owner deactivate, two reactivates, and a probe pair) all returned HTTP 200 with a full JSON body and correct `remaining_owners`. No 502, and the underlying rows matched the responses on a follow-up `roster` read. Nothing further to do | S |

Upheld in *Home Care Ass'n of America v. Weil*, 799 F.3d 1084 (D.C. Cir. 2015). **THE TRIGGER:** DOL NPRM at 90 FR 28976 (2025-07-02) proposes to **rescind the 2013 rule in its entirety** and "return to the 1975 regulations". It is at **Final Rule Stage** with a projected **November 2026** publication. If finalised as proposed, agency-employed companions become exempt from **both** minimum wage and overtime — a complete reversal. Even the narrower alternative under consideration removes exactly § 552.109 and § 552.6(b). **WHAT THIS MEANS FOR CODE:** current law is safe to encode and must NOT be a constant — exemption treatment wants a dated, configurable rule. **NEXT ACTION: re-check RIN 1235-AA51 and the FR Part 552 index before any release after October 2026.** Nothing to build now. The ROLE half alone appears at fifteen further sites across invoices, proposals, jobs and supplier documents. **Fixed:** `rfAuth.ownsRow(session, row)` and `rfAuth.seesAllRows(session)` in `api/rf-auth.js`, which is where the role sets live, so a future change to who counts as management lands beside the predicate that reads it. **ONLY THE PREDICATE WAS UNIFIED.** Each branch keeps its own not-provisioned and no-row policy — a read degrades to an empty list, a write refuses with 503 — and a test asserts those stay different. Unifying them would be a behaviour change dressed as a refactor, which is exactly what the original deferral was protecting against. **A LATENT TRAP FOUND WHILE DOING IT:** `MANAGEMENT_ROLES` ⊂ `BROAD_READ_ROLES` ({owner, admin} vs {owner, admin, estimator}), so `!MANAGEMENT && !BROAD` is today identical to `!BROAD` — the management term is redundant at all 22 sites. Both terms are kept, because the day a management role is added that is not a broad reader the redundant form silently changes meaning everywhere at once. A negative control proved no behavioural test can catch that today, so it is guarded structurally and by a subset assertion instead. **THE LIVE ROUND TRIPS ARE NOW DONE, 2026-09-04, on RF-AUDIT-2026** — which exists and works, correcting the earlier note here that it returned 401. Credential verified before being trusted: `check_license` 200, `login` 200 as `sairn-demo-owner` with role `owner`, `whoami` echoing the same identity. No exposed customer PIN was used. **A FIRST PASS PASSED 11/11 AND PROVED LESS THAN IT LOOKED LIKE** — RF-AUDIT-2026 had zero claims, so *read the photos of a real claim* and *reconcile a real claim* both SKIPPED and the gate's ALLOW path was never exercised at all. A green run that skips the substantive half is the shape this platform keeps getting caught by, so a fixture was created rather than reported around: `ZZ-GATE-JOB`, `ZZ-GATE-CLAIM` and a narrow-role employee `zz-gate-foreman`, on the audit licence, deliberately labelled and not deleted (there is no reachable delete path — its own row). **16/16 against the deployed endpoint**, covering both directions of every retrofitted branch: owner reads/reconciles/assesses a real claim; a foreman who is NOT the assignee is excluded from the list and gets 403 FORBIDDEN on photos, reconcile AND the write path (the seventh spelling); then the claim is assigned to that foreman and **the same three calls pass**. That last flip is what distinguishes a working gate from one that refuses everyone. Also caught live: the session header is `x-sd-auth`, not a bearer — a first probe sent it as `X-Session-Token` and every call answered 401 NO_SESSION, which would have read as a broken gate rather than a broken probe. Probes kept at `tools/rf_claim_gate_live_probe.py` and `tools/rf_roundtrip_probe.py`; credentials come from the environment, never the files. Equivalence remains proven exhaustively over all 64 role × employee × row combinations as well. **Remaining: the fifteen role-only sites**, out of scope here, count asserted by a test so this row cannot go stale the way the "four ways" figure did \| Phase 5 factored it into one `rfClaimGate` helper and used it for the three new branches, but deliberately did NOT retrofit the live 3b/3c branches (rf_claim_photos read/write, `reconcile`) — rewriting verified live code inside a feature commit is how a regression gets in unnoticed. `api/rf-auth.js`'s own header names duplicated role logic as SAIRNsenior's root cause, so this should not sit indefinitely. Retrofit + re-run the 3b/3c live round trips together | S |

Nobody came back to update this row, so it read as unstarted work. **But it is not fully closed either, and that half is real: `b044f35` shipped NO MIGRATION.** Every invoice and issued proposal written before 2026-08-26 still carries both keys and still misreports on every read — including `INV-00001` on `RF-PINNACLE-2026`, the row this entry cites as its own live evidence. **Reproduced 2026-09-01 against the real library, not inferred:** a stored blob of `{"tax_rate":7.5,"tax":787.5}` reads back `tax_basis:'amount'`, `tax_rate:null`, plus the invented *"both a tax rate and a tax amount were given"* warning; the same blob with `tax` removed reads `tax_basis:'rate'` with no warning, and **`tax` 787.5 / `total` 11287.5 are identical in both** — the money never moves, only the disclosure. **NEXT ACTION: run `sql/sairnroofing_tax_provenance_backfill_2026-09-01.sql`, section by section, section 0 first.** It removes the derived `tax` key only where the stored value is exactly what the rate re-derives, so a GENUINE rate/amount disagreement is deliberately left alone with its (true) warning intact. So an invoice priced purely from a rate reads back as `tax_basis:'amount'`, `tax_rate:null`, plus a spurious `problems:["both a tax rate and a tax amount were given; the amount was used"]` — a disagreement the user never expressed. **The money is correct and unaffected** (787.5 both ways); what is wrong is the provenance disclosure, on a codebase whose schema header explicitly argues derived figures must not misstate their own basis. Live evidence: INV-00001 on `RF-PINNACLE-2026`. Not folded into this verification pass, hence a row. Decide whether the stored `tax` is redundant when a rate is present, or whether `summarizeInvoice` should prefer the rate when both came from its own writer | S |

| **Platform** | ~~The seed push gate reads seed files from the **working tree**, not from the commits being pushed~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-03 (Hank)** — 38/38 probe, 9/9 negative controls | Hank | — | `tools/sairn_push_gate_hook.py` calls `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py`, which reads `sql/*.json` **off disk**. So the drift it reports describes HEAD's working tree, not the refspec. **Reproduced, not theorised:** pushing `b351b49` — an engine-only commit touching zero seed files — was DENIED naming 13 missing New Hampshire rules, because the *later* seed commit was sitting in HEAD. This is the exact ordering the gate doc itself prescribes (deploy the computation standard, then load, then push the seed), so **the gate currently denies the workflow it documents.** Fix shape, not chosen: derive the seed set from `git diff` over the pushed range rather than from disk — but note that the disk read is also what makes the check honest about what will be live, so the two readings need reconciling rather than one replacing the other. **Workaround that is not a bypass, and should be preferred to the override:** check out the engine commit detached and push from there — at that commit no seed exists on disk, live genuinely matches repo, and the gate passes on its own terms | S |
| **Platform** | ~~`SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off`, the gate's own documented override, is **unreachable from any Bash tool call**~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-03 (Hank)** — the inline prefix now works; a quoted mention still does not | Hank | — | `tools/sairn_push_gate_hook.py:287` reads `os.environ.get('SAIRN_SEED_GATE')` — the hook runs as a PreToolUse hook inside **Claude Code's** process, so it inherits Claude Code's environment, not the shell's. Prefixing `SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off git push ...` sets the variable in a child shell the hook never sees; it was tried and the push was denied identically. The override therefore only works if it is set in the environment Claude Code itself was launched with, which is not what CLAUDE.md's push protocol describes and not what the denial message tells the reader to do. **Consequence worth stating: the escape hatch every other gate here relies on does not exist for this one in practice**, so a session that hits a false denial has no documented way out. Fix shape, not chosen: read the override out of the hook's `tool_input.command` string (the same payload it already parses for the push refspec) so an inline prefix works, and correct the denial message either way. Related to the row above — they were found together and the working-tree bug is what makes this one bite | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **DECISION: where an EARLY-direction omission gets disclosed — `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` or the rule row's own note. Two seeded jurisdictions answer it one way and four answer it the other, and nothing says which is right** | **✅ DECIDED BY MICHAEL AND IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-01 (`a54fae9`), live-verified.** **Decision: `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` is the contract** — it is the channel the majority already used and the one the audit proved reliably carries the disclosure. Utah and Nevada are migrated onto it; their suites' assertions of the ABSENCE are replaced by assertions of the entry. **Second decision, same day: a LATE-direction disclosure is its own category.** Alabama moves from `direction: 'early'` to `'late'` and gains a structured `late_exposure` block (authority / summary / why_not_refused / caller_action), and the table's header now documents **DISCLOSED-LATE** as a deliberate third category beside REFUSED and DISCLOSED. `direction` is **worst-case, not typical**, and says so — Alabama's other two gaps are EARLY, but a caller switching on the field is asking *can this be late*. **A SECOND ONE CANNOT ARRIVE QUIETLY:** `coverageTableDefects()` runs over the table at module load and throws on `'late'` without the block, the block without `'late'`, a direction outside early/late, or a missing required key — exported so the invariant and its test are the same code. `deadline-coverage-contract.test.js` carries 25 assertions including that a REAL computation for `ut`, `nv` and `al` returns the disclosure. **Live: Utah and Nevada now return `coverage.direction: 'early'` where they previously returned none, and Alabama returns `'late'` with the block.** 167 tests pass | **Michael** | — | **THE TENSION IS REAL AND IS BETWEEN TWO DELIBERATE POSITIONS, not between a rule and an oversight.** Utah's and Nevada's gate docs both say their omissions are *disclosable* — Utah §8 is headed "Not modelled, all EARLY, all disclosable", Nevada's says "Safe, disclosable" — and both seeds then declare NO `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` entry, asserted verbatim by `deadline-utah.test.js:232` and `deadline-nevada.test.js:222` as *"its gaps are all EARLY and row-level"*. Alabama, Maryland, Missouri and Wisconsin took the opposite view and carry jurisdiction-level entries for omissions of the same EARLY class. **WHAT I CHECKED AND WHAT IT SHOWS:** "row-level" is a weaker claim than the assertion's wording implies — only **2 of Utah's 9 rows and 2 of Nevada's 10** carry any omission-flavoured `authority.note`, and the ones sampled explain rule STRUCTURE (which limb applies, why a row is separate) rather than disclosing the clerk-inaccessibility, hours-unit or filing-cutoff omissions the gate docs named. So a caller computing a Utah date is told nothing about them through either channel. **WHY THIS IS A DECISION AND NOT A FIX, and why I reverted rather than shipped:** adding the two entries is three minutes of work and it silently overrides a choice another session made and locked; it broke 16 suites, which is the mechanism working as intended. **The question for Michael is which channel is the contract** — if `coverage` is what a caller reads, Utah and Nevada need entries and their tests need updating; if the row note is, then four jurisdictions are over-disclosing and the gate docs should stop saying "disclosable" when they mean something else. **No date is wrong either way — every omission at issue runs EARLY** | S |
| **Tooling** | ~~The post-push deploy hook compares live against **LOCAL HEAD**~~ — **FIXED, and then fixed again for a second cause** | **CLOSED 2026-09-02 (Cody).** `63d67ad` changed the baseline to `origin/main`, fetched **after** the 60s wait so a push landing from another clone during the wait moves the baseline instead of registering as drift, and added a line saying whether THIS clone is in step — "deploy stuck" and "your clone is stale" need opposite responses. Verified against a real stale-HEAD scenario rather than a live check, because with everything in sync both baselines agree and the test discriminates nothing: with HEAD moved back one stonedesk commit, the OLD baseline reports drift and the NEW one still agrees with live. **Then it false-alarmed again** on 2026-09-02 and the second cause was different: it fired on a push containing only `sql/`, `tools/` and `tests/` while ANOTHER clone's real `stonedesk.html` change was mid-deploy — real drift belonging to someone else's push, attributed to one that could not have caused it. `0a17486` scopes it to pushes that actually shipped `stonedesk.html`, using `origin/main@{1}..origin/main`, a range deliberately WIDER than "what I just pushed" so it can only suppress a check that could not concern that file. **Both fixes were found by the diagnostic line the first fix added**, which is the argument for hooks that explain themselves. Original status: Open — **fired twice on 2026-09-01 (Hank**), both false** | unassigned | — | `tools/deploy_verify_notify.py` hashes `sairn.vercel.app/stonedesk` against `HEAD:stonedesk.html` ~60s after a push and reports a possible stuck Vercel webhook. **Both firings today were a RACE, not a stuck webhook**, and neither push touched `stonedesk.html`: live matched **`origin/main`** byte-for-byte on each occasion while local HEAD was one commit behind, because another session had pushed StoneDesk work seconds later. **Its recommended remedy is actively wrong in this case** — it advises pushing a trivial re-trigger commit, which would add a no-op commit to a shared branch to fix nothing. **In a four-clone setup a local HEAD is stale by default**, so this will keep firing. Fix shape, not chosen: fetch and compare against `origin/main` rather than `HEAD`, and say which ref was used in the message. **Diagnose it by comparing the three hashes** — `HEAD:stonedesk.html`, `origin/main:stonedesk.html` and live — before believing it; if live equals origin/main the deploy is fine | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | `service_extension.order` is carried on 135 seeded rows in four different vocabularies and is **read by nothing** | **DECIDED BY MICHAEL AND DONE 2026-09-02 (`f0723f1`), reloaded and live-verified.** **Decision: DELETE the field platform-wide rather than build a validator for it** — the existing values are self-contradictory across rows, the same label sitting on standards whose sequences are opposite operations, so a validator could not fix what is already wrong without 135 rows being corrected by hand first. **129 keys removed from 22 files.** Confirmed unread first: every `.js`, `.py` and `.html` in the repo outside `node_modules`, `.git` and `archive/` grepped for a property access or key read of `order` — eight hits, all unrelated (a SAIRNcare pharmacy order, `opts.order` in `med-schedule.js`, StoneDesk's order-cart CSS and route-optimiser array, a Supabase `.order()` call, and a comment). **AND PROVEN INERT AGAINST DATES, WHICH A GREP CANNOT DO:** all 184 rules carrying a `service_extension` computed under all eight recognised methods across seven trigger dates chosen to land on weekends and holidays — **10,304 cells, zero changed, zero added, zero removed.** Reloaded: the load-state check reported exactly **129 stale entries** beforehand, matching the count removed, and MISSING 0 STALE 0 EXTRA 0 after at 407 rules. Live spot-checks return the same dates and extension states as before. **One process note worth keeping: the first removal pass left 17 stray carriage returns in four files** — the pattern matched the line feed inside a CRLF — and the files still parsed and every test still passed. The only symptom was four diffs of 300-400 lines instead of 14. **Caught by checking the diff SHAPE, not the test result.** | **Michael** | — | Grepped before adding a 136th: **neither `api/_lib/deadline-engine.js` nor `api/legal-deadlines.js` ever reads `service_extension.order`.** The sequencing that actually runs comes from the STANDARD's own `sequence` field — `add_to_period_then_roll` or `roll_then_add_then_roll` — which is declared once per standard and is genuinely load-bearing. **The row-level field is inert documentation, and the four vocabularies in use do not agree with each other:** `after_base_period` on 64 rows, `after_period_expires` on 30, `after` on 30, `add_to_period` on 11, and **49 rows carry none at all**. **THE RISK IS NOT THE REDUNDANCY, IT IS THAT NOTHING CHECKS IT AGAINST THE SEQUENCE THAT RUNS.** A row reading `order: 'after'` sits on standards whose sequence is `add_to_period_then_roll` — which adds the days INSIDE the period and rolls once — and on standards whose sequence is `roll_then_add_then_roll`, which is the opposite operation. Someone reading a seed to understand a date can be told the wrong thing by a field that looks authoritative and is not. **DELAWARE CARRIES NO `order` KEY, deliberately**, and its `_readme` says why rather than leaving the absence to look like an oversight. **Fix shape, not chosen — and it is a decision because both directions are defensible:** either delete the field from all 135 rows as noise, or make it real by having the validator reject a row whose `order` contradicts its standard's `sequence`. **What must NOT happen is a third state adding one by copying a neighbour**, which is how four vocabularies became normal | S |

## What this index does NOT cover, stated so the gap is known

- **Per-app handoffs were not read line by line.** Rows come from
  `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md` (section-by-section), `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md`, and work
  done in-session on 2026-08-23. There are ~40 handoff files; an item that
  exists **only** inside one and never reached the backlog is not here yet.
  That is the first thing to improve on the next rebuild.
- **Sizing is judgment, not estimation.** S/M/L is a rough shape, not a
  commitment.
- **Owners are observed, not assigned.** `unassigned` means no session has
  claimed it in the active-work log (`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md` at rebuild time;
  now the three per-session files) — not that it is unimportant. Rows
  owned by **Michael** need a decision or an access level a session does not
  have.
- **⚠️ rows carry a number or claim this rebuild could not confirm.** They say
  so in Next action.

---

## Keeping it alive

This file rots the moment it stops being updated, and a stale index is worse
than none — it makes work look handled when it is not.

1. **When you close something, move the row** to *Recently closed* with the
   commit SHA. Do not delete it silently.
2. **When you find something new, add a row** in the same action as logging it
   in `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`. Two places, one action, or they diverge.
3. **Re-derive, don't trust.** Before a planning decision, spot-check the rows
   you are about to rely on. This rebuild found one stale backlog entry
   (SAIRNlaw trust disbursement, listed open, actually shipped) out of 26 —
   assume a similar rate next time.
4. **Do not renumber or restructure the backlog to match this file.** The
   backlog is the detailed record; this is the index over it.
